> Sub-tolerance features remain unchanged across both approaches. The connectivity invariant still matters — inversion and overlap resolution can disconnect regions through remnant stripping, requiring the same re-split invariant that partition-first gets by construction, though relabeling can also disconnect and needs re-splitting too. Overall, the inversion approach is a major step forward from the old pipeline, capturing the most critical fix: per-seed cross-type competition eliminates stage ordering and the worst fragmentation pathologies, while keeping the fit-driven boundary placement that handles tangent junctions robustly without relying on discrete curvature estimates.
> 
> The inversion is also a smaller code change — it keeps the existing BFS, fitters, and seed machinery mostly intact, just restructuring the driver loop and selection logic. But it still falls short of partition-first in a few ways: fit conditioning from tiny seeds remains heuristic-heavy with rescue ladders rather than fitting maximal regions, freeform surfaces still gather leftovers with accidental analytic confetti absorption, and overlap resolution plus acceptance guards still need degenerate fit guards and tie-breaks, so the heuristic surface shrinks but doesn't fully disappear.

> The expansion cost per region is about 5× (bounded and much better than old tiling, actually comparable to the old pipeline's total), and curvature seed-ordering is still needed for efficiency and first-commit quality. Where the inversion actually wins is in tangent-junction boundary placement: it's fit-driven, uses exact vertex distances, and doesn't depend on discrete curvature jump thresholds like J or κ_floor calibration, whereas partition-first's approach to this is still being worked out.
> 
> The inversion also handles curvature-estimate noise more gracefully since ordering plays only a supporting role, and it's closer to the existing code so there's less risk of a long red-suite period. The shared-face and junction-ambiguity idea maps naturally to reality since junction vertices genuinely lie on both surfaces. Interestingly, partition-first could adopt fit-driven boundary refinement (it already does through relabeling), and the inversion could adopt region-level refit after resolution — these approaches aren't mutually exclusive.
> 
> The inversion is essentially RANSAC-like local best-model region growing, while partition-first is segmentation plus model selection; a hybrid could use partition-first as the primary strategy and invoke the split probe as a repair mechanism when classification fails. The efficiency estimate holds up: skipping faces with good existing hypotheses as seed faces, combined with curvature ordering, means the number of seed rounds approximates the number of regions plus failures. One remaining concern is the nosecone_truncated case where a bogus hypothesis split the real cone's BFS into two fragments — in the inversion approach, seeding from the cone's interior would let it expand naturally without that false split.
> 
> With the inversion, an interior seed expands to cover the whole cone since no competing claims block it yet, yielding a single winning hypothesis and preventing fragmentation structurally. The key safeguard is that claimed faces can't become seeds, so once the sphere commits its interior faces, no later hypothesis can re-derive that same region even if expansion could theoretically consume already-claimed faces.
> 
> The expansion into claimed territory is bounded by vertex tolerance—only genuinely ambiguous faces get overlapped—and when conflicts do arise, I resolve them per-face by selecting the best-fit hypothesis, then re-splitting connected components and refitting. This is essentially a streamlined version of the earlier approach. The verdict is that this would work substantially better than the old pipeline.
> 
> The main advantage is fixing the headline fragmentation and stage-ordering problems with minimal code changes. Cross-type competition per seed is sound under exactness since accidental fits lose by area and leakage across junctions stays bounded to true-ambiguity rows. The weaknesses remain: ill-conditioned small-seed fits still need rescue heuristics, freeform becomes a leftover-gathering afterthought with the accidental-confetti problem, and I still need overlap resolution, degeneracy guards, and tie-breaks. If edge classification calibration is a concern, this is the strongest fallback design. NURBS surfaces add another wrinkle since they're not directly seedable. The real takeaway is that both methods are converging conceptually: the inversion-based approach discovers regions through fit growth rather than edge classification, making the fit itself the classifier. I'd document this as the contingency plan and note where the two strategies align.


## 🤖 Assistant
<!-- msg_011Cd8E87UqtWD58TpPdpiRu -->

This inverted-loop design is a genuinely strong alternative — it's the best incremental redesign of the old pipeline I can construct, and under the exactness assumption its core decision rule is actually *sound*, not heuristic. Here's my honest assessment of how it would behave.

## Why the core rule works

The load-bearing property is what you might call the sub-region lemma: an accidental fit (the 6-face cylinder band on the nosecone sphere, a torus through a band of sphere quads) is by definition supported only on a *subset* of a true region, while the true surface fitted from an interior seed expands — with vertex tolerance as the arbiter — to the *entire* region. So when both compete on the same seed, the true surface always covers at least as much area, and largest-area-wins picks it. The old pipeline compared hypotheses born from *different* seeds at *different* times, which is why commit order mattered and fragmentation happened. Comparing all types on the *same* seed makes the comparison fair, and:

- **Fragmentation dies.** The nosecone_truncated 31-face split fragment can't form: expansion is allowed through claimed faces, so the interior-seeded cone covers the whole cone in one trial regardless of what committed earlier.
- **Stage ordering dies.** Per-seed competition replaces the planar→spherical→toroidal→conical sequencing and its exclusion rules.
- **The tiling pathology dies too.** The 429-second pill_fine case came from *thousands* of seeds each growing a bogus torus band on the sphere. Here, the sphere's interior seed runs one torus expansion (one band), one cone expansion (one band), loses them to the sphere, and the whole region leaves the seed pool. Cost per region ≈ 5 bounded expansions. The large_sphere mask becomes unnecessary without any masking.
- **Cross-region leakage is bounded.** My first instinct was that a fitted surface could span two regions (the tangent cone of a sphere *is* the real adjacent cone), but vertex tolerance at 5e-5 limits overlap to faces whose vertices genuinely lie on both surfaces — the single junction row. Two distinct analytic surfaces agreeing to 5e-5 over an extended 2D patch would be near-identical. So the shared-face semantics maps exactly onto the real ambiguity, which is elegant.

One more real advantage over the partition approach: **boundary placement never depends on discrete curvature estimates.** The capsule's equal-radius sphere↔cylinder junction, which forces my 2.1 design into a two-sided curvature descriptor with calibrated thresholds (J, κ_floor), is handled here by exact vertex distances — the fit itself finds the boundary. Curvature ordering is only used to pick seeds, where an error costs efficiency, not correctness. That's a graceful degradation the partition approach doesn't have; its miscalibrations must be caught and repaired explicitly.

## Where it falls short of the documented plan

**1. Small-seed fit conditioning is unsolved — and it's what pipe_elbow actually needs.** The inversion changes *when* types compete but not *what the fitter sees*: still a 1-ring or 3-face fan. On the pipe elbow's strip tessellation, a 1-ring torus fit is ill-conditioned (no major-arc coverage), so the torus expansion fails at every seed, the cylinder wins a strip, and the elbow decomposes into cylinder strips again — the exact failure that spawned strip-merge seeding. Fixing it inside this design means re-adding escalation machinery (e.g. "when growth stalls, refit the stalled frontier with a higher-DOF type using the already-grown support" — a model-promotion variant your spec doesn't include but would need). Partition-first fits the whole elbow tube at once; conditioning is solved structurally.

**2. Freeform stays a second-class afterthought.** NURBS can't participate in the per-seed competition (you can't fit a B-spline patch from a 1-ring; parameterization needs a bounded region). So on a freeform surface, the winner at each seed is the largest *accidental analytic* patch — the 12-face cylinder ribbons on the saddle, the accidental coplanar strips — and you're back to gathering leftovers and absorbing ≤16-face analytic confetti afterward, with the guards that entails. Partition-first hands the freeform fitter an intact region.

**3. The heuristic surface shrinks a lot but doesn't collapse.** You still need: overlap resolution into a strict partition for stage 3 (per-face best-fit among claimants — essentially the 2.9 rule — plus the connected-component re-split, since resolution can still disconnect a remnant; face 5841's family is about *selection stripping faces*, and any design with overlaps keeps a small version of that step); degeneracy guards and a DOF tiebreak (on a plane region, a near-infinite-radius cylinder and sphere *tie* the plane on area — "largest" alone is ill-defined without simplest-on-ties, plus the existing max-radius/half-angle guards); a crisp definition of "already has a good hypothesis" for seed suppression; and the curvature ordering itself, which remains load-bearing for keeping first-commit quality high.

**4. Cost is ~5 expansions per region.** Much better than the old pathologies, likely somewhat worse than partition-first's one-classification-per-region with fits that mostly succeed on the first or second rung. Not a deciding factor.

## Bottom line

I'd rank it clearly above the old pipeline and honestly competitive with partition-first — the two converge more than they first appear: your inversion effectively *discovers* regions by fit growth with the fit as classifier, where partition-first *extracts* regions from edge signals and then classifies. The trade is: the inversion buys exact, calibration-free boundaries at tangent junctions and a much smaller code delta, at the price of keeping small-seed conditioning heuristics and leaving freeform as leftover-gathering. Partition-first buys whole-region fit conditioning and first-class freeform, at the price of a calibrated edge classifier whose mistakes must be explicitly repaired.

If the 2.1 curvature calibration turns out to be the weak link in practice (coarse fillets, f32 quantization noise), this inversion is exactly the design I'd retreat to — and it's compatible with keeping most of the new plan's skeleton (the repair loop, invariants, and per-region refit all apply to its output unchanged). It would be worth a short "Contingency: seed-first multi-type competition" note in the plan's Abandoned Approaches-adjacent territory — not abandoned, just not chosen — so the option and its rationale don't get lost. Want me to add that?

## Contingency: Seed-First Multi-Type Competition

Not abandoned — considered (2026-07-17) and not chosen. This is the fallback

tuning). Recorded so the option and its rationale don't get lost.

**The design**: keep fit-driven BFS growth, but invert the old pipeline's
loops. Iterate over seed faces in curvature-score order (interior faces
first — the existing seed-ordering heuristics); skip faces already claimed
by a committed hypothesis. For each seed, run *every* surface type's
fit-and-expand from that same seed, and commit the hypothesis with the
largest supported area (with a simplest-DOF tiebreak). Expansion may grow
through already-claimed faces — junction faces whose vertices genuinely lie
on two surfaces end up claimed by both — but claimed faces never seed. After
all faces are claimed, resolve overlaps into a strict partition (per-face
best-fit among claimants, the boundary-relabel rule) and re-split any
disconnected remnant.

**Why it is sound, not heuristic** (under the exactness assumption): an
accidental fit is by definition supported on a *sub-region* — the true
surface fitted from an interior seed expands, with vertex tolerance as the
arbiter, to the entire region, so when all types compete on the *same* seed,
largest-area-wins selects the true surface. The old pipeline compared
hypotheses born from different seeds at different times, which is why commit
order mattered. Cross-region leakage is bounded: two distinct analytic
surfaces agreeing to vertex tolerance over an extended patch would be
near-identical, so overlap is confined to genuine junction rows.

**What it fixes without any masks or ordering**: fragmentation (an
interior-seeded surface covers its whole region regardless of prior
commits), stage ordering (per-seed competition replaces it), and the
accidental-fit tiling cost (each region pays ~5 bounded expansions once,
not per-face — the large_sphere pathology disappears without masking). Its
distinctive strength over partition-then-classify: **boundary placement
never depends on discrete curvature estimates** — the fit itself finds the
boundary from exact vertex distances (the capsule's equal-radius
sphere↔cylinder junction needs no two-sided descriptor here), and curvature
ordering errors cost only efficiency, never correctness.

**Why it was not chosen**:
1. Small-seed fit conditioning is unsolved — fits still start from 1-rings/
   fans, so the pipe-elbow torus (strip tessellation; a 1-ring spans no
   major arc) again decomposes into cylinder strips unless model-promotion
   machinery ("when growth stalls, refit the stalled frontier at higher
   DOF using the grown support") is added back. Partition-first fits the
   whole region at once.
2. Freeform stays second-class: a B-spline patch cannot compete from a seed
   (parameterization needs a bounded region), so freeform surfaces get
   tiled by their largest accidental analytic patches and the old
   leftover-gathering + confetti-absorption heuristics return.
3. The residual heuristic surface is larger: overlap resolution + remnant
   re-split, degeneracy guards and the DOF tiebreak (on a plane region a
   near-infinite-radius cylinder *ties* the plane on area), a "good enough
   to suppress seeding" definition, and load-bearing seed ordering.

If adopted, the 2.4 repair loop, the post-stage-2 invariants, and the 2.5
output contract apply to its results unchanged — only 2.1–2.3 would be
replaced by the seed-competition driver.

Done. The plan now has a **"Contingency: Seed-First Multi-Type Competition"** section, placed between the Rewrite Roadmap and Abandoned Approaches so it reads as "considered and held in reserve" rather than retired. It records:

- **The design** — your inverted-loop spec: curvature-ordered seeds, all surface types fit-and-expanded from each seed, largest-area winner with a simplest-DOF tiebreak, expansion allowed through claimed faces (junction faces claimable by both sides), claimed faces never seeding, and a final overlap-resolution + remnant re-split pass.
- **Why it's sound** — the sub-region lemma: accidental fits are sub-region by definition, so same-seed competition always favors the true surface, with cross-region leakage bounded to genuine junction rows by vertex tolerance.
- **What it fixes for free** — fragmentation, stage ordering, and the tiling cost, plus its distinctive strength: boundaries come from exact vertex distances, never from calibrated curvature thresholds.
- **Why it wasn't chosen** — small-seed fit conditioning (the pipe-elbow strip problem), freeform remaining a leftover-gathering afterthought, and the larger residual heuristic surface.
- **Compatibility** — the 2.4 repair loop, invariants, and 2.5 output contract apply to its results unchanged; only 2.1–2.3 would swap out.

I also wired the trigger into Rewrite Roadmap step 1: the `J`/`κ_floor` calibration step is now explicitly the decision gate — if no calibration yields reliable partitions across the corpus, switch to the contingency before building 2.3/2.4 on bad partitions. Memory is updated to match.

1	use clap::{ArgAction, Parser, ValueEnum};
2	use opencascade_sys::{top_abs, top_exp};

5	// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
6	// Stage
7	// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
8	
9	/// Stage identifier as (major, minor). Minor=0 means run all sub-stages of that major stage.
10	/// Examples: (1, 2) = stage 1.2, (2, 0) = all of stage 2, (4, 1) = full pipeline.
11	#[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy, PartialEq)]
12	pub struct Stage(pub u8, pub u8);
13	
14	impl Stage {
15	    /// The final sub-stage for each major stage.
16	    fn last_minor(major: u8) -> u8 {
17	        match major {
18	            1 => 3,
19	            2 => 9,
20	            3 => 6,
21	            4 => 1,
22	            _ => 0,
23	        }
24	    }
25	
26	    /// Normalize: if minor is 0, expand to last sub-stage of that major.
27	    pub fn normalized(self) -> (u8, u8) {
28	        if self.1 == 0 {
29	            (self.0, Self::last_minor(self.0))
30	        } else {
31	            (self.0, self.1)
32	        }
33	    }
34	
35	    /// Returns true if the pipeline should run at least through the given stage.
36	    pub fn at_least(&self, major: u8, minor: u8) -> bool {
37	        let (sm, ss) = self.normalized();
38	        (sm, ss) >= (major, minor)
39	    }
40	}
41	
42	impl Default for Stage {
43	    fn default() -> Self {
44	        Stage(4, 1)
45	    }
46	}
47	
48	fn parse_stage(s: &str) -> Result<Stage, String> {
49	    if let Some((major_s, minor_s)) = s.split_once('.') {
50	        let major: u8 = major_s.parse().map_err(|_| format!("invalid stage: {s}"))?;
51	        let minor: u8 = minor_s.parse().map_err(|_| format!("invalid stage: {s}"))?;
52	        if !(1..=4).contains(&major) || !(1..=Stage::last_minor(major)).contains(&minor) {
53	            return Err(format!("invalid stage: {s} (valid: 1.1–4.1)"));
54	        }
55	        Ok(Stage(major, minor))
56	    } else {
57	        let major: u8 = s.parse().map_err(|_| format!("invalid stage: {s}"))?;
58	        if !(1..=4).contains(&major) {
59	            return Err(format!("invalid stage: {s} (valid: 1–4)"));
60	        }
61	        Ok(Stage(major, 0))
62	    }
63	}
64	
65	// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
66	// Units
67	// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
68	
69	/// Length units for STL/STEP files.
70	#[derive(Debug, Default, Clone, Copy, PartialEq, ValueEnum)]
71	pub enum Units {
72	    /// Millimeters
73	    #[default]
74	    Mm,
75	    /// Centimeters
76	    Cm,
77	    /// Meters
78	    M,
79	    /// Inches
80	    In,
81	    /// Feet
82	    Ft,
83	    /// Micrometers
84	    Um,
85	}
86	
87	impl Units {
88	    /// Conversion factor: 1 of this unit = N millimeters.
89	    pub fn to_mm(self) -> f64 {
90	        match self {
91	            Units::Mm => 1.0,
92	            Units::Cm => 10.0,
93	            Units::M => 1000.0,
94	            Units::In => 25.4,
95	            Units::Ft => 304.8,
96	            Units::Um => 0.001,
97	        }
98	    }
99	}
100	
101	
102	impl Display for Units {
103	    fn fmt(&self, f: &mut Formatter<'_>) -> std::fmt::Result {
104	        match self {
105	            Units::Mm => write!(f, "mm"),
106	            Units::Cm => write!(f, "cm"),
107	            Units::M => write!(f, "m"),
108	            Units::In => write!(f, "in"),
109	            Units::Ft => write!(f, "ft"),
110	            Units::Um => write!(f, "μm"),
111	        }
112	    }
113	}

119	/// brepper — Convert STL mesh to STEP with fitted analytic and freeform surfaces.
120	#[derive(Parser, Debug)]
121	#[command(name = "brepper", version, about)]
122	struct CliArgs {
123	    /// Input STL file (binary or ASCII).
124	    pub input_stl: String,
125	
126	    /// Output STEP file.
127	    #[arg(short = 'o', long = "output")]
128	    pub output_step: Option<String>,
129	
130	    /// Units used by the STL file.
131	    #[arg(long, default_value = "mm", value_enum)]
132	    pub stl_units: Units,
133	
134	    /// Units to use in exported STEP file.
135	    #[arg(long, default_value = "mm", value_enum)]
136	    pub step_units: Units,
137	
138	    /// STEP file to compare against at each stage.
139	    #[arg(long = "compare")]
140	    pub compare_step: Option<String>,
141	
142	    /// Fitting tolerance for vertex-to-surface distance, in STL units.
143	    #[arg(long, default_value = "5e-5")]
144	    pub vertex_tolerance: f64,
145	
146	    /// Tolerance for surface-to-triangle-face offset, in STL units.
147	    #[arg(long, default_value = "0.4")]
148	    pub surface_tolerance: f64,
149	
150	    /// Max dihedral angle between adjacent triangles on same surface, in
151	    /// degrees. Medium-resolution CAD exports step small-radius fillets up
152	    /// to ~24° between rows (Onshape "medium" enforces chordal deviation, so
153	    /// small features get few segments regardless of the angular preset);
154	    /// the default must admit those.
155	    #[arg(long, default_value = "25")]
156	    pub angular_tolerance: f64,
157	
158	    /// Increment verbosity by 1 per flag (-v = 1, -vv = 2, -vvv = 3).
159	    #[arg(short = 'v', action = ArgAction::Count)]
160	    pub verbose_short: u8,
161	
162	    /// Set verbosity to an explicit level:
163	    /// --verbose=1 (stage summaries),
164	    /// --verbose=2 (per-hypothesis details),
165	    /// --verbose=3 (per-committed-hypothesis seed/count info, stats, profiles),
166	    /// --verbose=4 (per-seed-attempt details),
167	    /// --verbose=5 (per-BFS-face exploration details).
168	    /// Bare --verbose sets level 1.
169	    #[arg(long = "verbose", value_name = "LEVEL", default_missing_value = "1", num_args = 0..=1)]
170	    pub verbose_level: Option<u8>,
171	
172	    /// Suppress non-error output.
173	    #[arg(short = 'q', long)]
174	    pub quiet: bool,
175	
176	    /// Fail on degenerate mesh faces instead of repairing them.
177	    #[arg(long)]
178	    pub strict_mesh: bool,
179	
180	    /// Enable debug output and intermediate files.
181	    #[arg(long)]
182	    pub debug: bool,
183	
184	    /// Stop after this stage (e.g. "2.2" or "2"). Default: run all stages.
185	    #[arg(long, value_parser = parse_stage)]
186	    pub stage: Option<Stage>,
187	
188	    /// Comma-separated list of sub-stages to skip entirely (e.g.
189	    /// "2.2,2.3,2.5,2.7" disables conical, spherical, toroidal, and NURBS
190	    /// recognition, leaving every mesh face to fall back to a planar
191	    /// hypothesis). Unlike --stage, which stops the pipeline early, skipped
192	    /// stages simply do nothing and the rest of the pipeline (selection,
193	    /// stage 3, stage 4) still runs through to output. Honored sub-stages:
194	    /// 2.2 (conical), 2.3 (spherical), 2.5 (toroidal), 2.7 (NURBS),
195	    /// 2.8 (redundant-surface absorption), 2.9 (boundary refinement).
196	    #[arg(long = "skip-stage", value_parser = parse_stage, value_delimiter = ',')]
197	    pub skip_stages: Option<Vec<Stage>>,
198	
199	    /// Comma-separated list of stages for interactive visualization (e.g. "2.2,3.1").
200	    #[arg(long = "viz-stages", value_parser = parse_stage, value_delimiter = ',')]
201	    pub viz_stages: Option<Vec<Stage>>,
202	
203	    /// Detail level for viz-stages visualization pauses:
204	    /// 1 = pause at committed hypotheses and stage 2.6 selections (default),
205	    /// 2 = also pause at each accepted seed,
206	    /// 3 = also pause at each BFS face acceptance.
207	    #[arg(long = "viz-stages-level", default_value = "1")]
208	    pub viz_stages_level: u8,
209	}

215	/// Resolved configuration used by the pipeline. Tolerances are stored in mm.
216	pub struct Config {
217	    /// Input STL file path.
218	    pub input_stl: String,
219	    /// Output STEP file path.
220	    pub output_step: Option<String>,
221	    /// Units of the input STL file.
222	    pub stl_units: Units,
223	    /// Units for the output STEP file.
224	    pub step_units: Units,
225	    /// STEP file path to compare against at each stage.
226	    pub compare_step: Option<String>,
227	    /// Shape loaded from the --compare STEP file, for bounded distance checks.
228	    pub compare_shape: Option<opencascade_sys::OwnedPtr<opencascade_sys::topo_ds::Shape>>,
229	    /// Number of faces in the compare shape (for status output).
230	    pub compare_face_count: usize,
231	    /// Fitting tolerance for vertex-to-surface distance, in mm.
232	    pub vertex_tolerance_mm: f64,
233	    /// Tolerance for surface-to-triangle-face offset, in mm.
234	    pub surface_tolerance_mm: f64,
235	    /// Max dihedral angle (radians) between adjacent triangles on the same surface.
236	    pub angular_tolerance_rad: f64,
237	    /// Verbosity level (0 = quiet, 1 = summaries, 2 = hypotheses, 3 = commit details, 4 = seed details, 5 = BFS trace).
238	    pub verbosity: u8,
239	    /// Convenience: verbosity >= 1.
240	    pub verbose: bool,
241	    /// Suppress non-error output.
242	    pub quiet: bool,
243	    /// Fail on degenerate mesh faces instead of repairing them.
244	    pub strict_mesh: bool,
245	    /// Enable debug output and intermediate files.
246	    pub debug: bool,
247	    /// Stop after this stage.
248	    pub stage: Stage,
249	    /// Sub-stages to skip entirely (produce zero hypotheses of that type)
250	    /// while still running the rest of the pipeline through to output.
251	    pub skip_stages: Vec<Stage>,
252	    /// Stages to visualize interactively.
253	    pub viz_stages: Vec<Stage>,
254	    /// Detail level for viz-stages visualization (1=commits, 2=seeds, 3=BFS faces).
255	    pub viz_stages_level: u8,
256	}

259	    fn fmt(&self, f: &mut Formatter<'_>) -> std::fmt::Result {
260	        f.debug_struct("Config")
261	            .field("input_stl", &self.input_stl)
262	            .field("output_step", &self.output_step)

264	            .field("step_units", &self.step_units)

267	            .field("vertex_tolerance_mm", &self.vertex_tolerance_mm)
268	            .field("surface_tolerance_mm", &self.surface_tolerance_mm)
269	            .field("angular_tolerance_rad", &self.angular_tolerance_rad)
270	            .field("verbosity", &self.verbosity)
271	            .field("verbose", &self.verbose)
272	            .field("quiet", &self.quiet)
273	            .field("strict_mesh", &self.strict_mesh)
274	            .field("debug", &self.debug)
275	            .field("stage", &self.stage)
276	            .field("skip_stages", &self.skip_stages)
277	            .field("viz_stages", &self.viz_stages)
278	            .field("viz_stages_level", &self.viz_stages_level)
279	            .finish()

282	
283	impl Config {
284	    fn from_cli(args: CliArgs) -> Self {
285	        let scale = args.stl_units.to_mm();
286	        Self {
287	            input_stl: args.input_stl,
288	            output_step: args.output_step,
289	            stl_units: args.stl_units,
290	            step_units: args.step_units,
291	            compare_step: args.compare_step,
292	            compare_shape: None,
293	            compare_face_count: 0,
294	            vertex_tolerance_mm: args.vertex_tolerance * scale,
295	            surface_tolerance_mm: args.surface_tolerance * scale,
296	            angular_tolerance_rad: args.angular_tolerance.to_radians(),
297	            verbosity: args.verbose_level.unwrap_or(0).max(args.verbose_short),
298	            verbose: args.verbose_level.unwrap_or(0).max(args.verbose_short) >= 1,
299	            quiet: args.quiet,
300	            strict_mesh: args.strict_mesh,
301	            debug: args.debug,
302	            stage: args.stage.unwrap_or_default(),
303	            skip_stages: args.skip_stages.unwrap_or_default(),
304	            viz_stages: args.viz_stages.unwrap_or_default(),
305	            viz_stages_level: args.viz_stages_level,
306	        }
307	    }
308	
309	    /// Check if a specific stage should be visualized.
310	    pub fn viz_active(&self, major: u8, minor: u8) -> bool {
311	        self.viz_stages.iter().any(|s| {
312	            let (sm, ss) = s.normalized();
313	            sm == major && ss == minor
314	        })
315	    }
316	
317	    /// Check if a specific sub-stage was named in --skip-stage. Matches the
318	    /// exact (major, minor) pair as parsed — unlike \`viz_active\`/\`at_least\`,
319	    /// a bare major (minor 0) is not expanded, since the recognition
320	    /// sub-stages this is meant for (2.2, 2.3, 2.5, 2.7) always need an
321	    /// explicit minor.
322	    pub fn skip_stage(&self, major: u8, minor: u8) -> bool {
323	        self.skip_stages.iter().any(|s| s.0 == major && s.1 == minor)
324	    }
325	
326	    /// If --compare was specified, load the STEP file shape for bounded distance comparison.
327	    pub fn load_compare_step(&mut self) -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
328	        let step_path = match &self.compare_step {
329	            Some(p) => p.clone(),
330	            None => return Ok(()),
331	        };
332	
333	        let step_shape = crate::read_step_file(&step_path)
334	            .map_err(|e| -> Box<dyn std::error::Error> { e.into() })?;
335	
336	        // Count faces for validation and status output
337	        let mut face_count = 0_usize;
338	        let mut explorer = top_exp::Explorer::new_shape_shapeenum2(
339	            &step_shape,
340	            top_abs::ShapeEnum::Face,
341	            top_abs::ShapeEnum::Shape,
342	        );
343	        while explorer.more() {
344	            face_count += 1;
345	            explorer.next();
346	        }
347	
348	        if face_count == 0 {
349	            return Err(format!("STEP file {step_path} contains no faces").into());
350	        }
351	
352	        if !self.quiet {
353	            eprintln!("Loaded {} faces from comparison STEP file", face_count);
354	        }
355	
356	        self.compare_face_count = face_count;
357	        self.compare_shape = Some(step_shape);
358	
359	        Ok(())
360	    }
361	}
362	/// Parse command-line arguments and return a resolved Config.
363	pub fn parse_config() -> Config {
364	    Config::from_cli(CliArgs::parse())
365	}
366	
367	/// Parse from an explicit argument iterator (for testing or embedding).
368	pub fn parse_config_from<I, T>(args: I) -> Result<Config, clap::Error>
369	where
370	    I: IntoIterator<Item = T>,
371	    T: Into<std::ffi::OsString> + Clone,
372	{
373	    CliArgs::try_parse_from(args).map(Config::from_cli)
374	}

380	#[cfg(test)]
381	mod tests {
382	    use super::*;
383	
384	    fn parse(args: &[&str]) -> Config {
385	        let mut full = vec!["brepper"];
386	        full.extend_from_slice(args);
387	        parse_config_from(full).expect("parse should succeed")
388	    }
389	
390	    #[test]
391	    fn minimal_args() {
392	        let config = parse(&["input.stl"]);
393	        assert_eq!(config.input_stl, "input.stl");
394	        assert_eq!(config.stage, Stage(4, 1));
395	        assert!(!config.verbose);
396	    }
397	
398	    #[test]
399	    fn full_args() {
400	        let config = parse(&[
401	            "input.stl", "-o", "output.step", "--compare", "ref.step",
402	            "--stage=2.3", "-v", "--vertex-tolerance=1e-6",
403	        ]);
404	        assert_eq!(config.input_stl, "input.stl");
405	        assert_eq!(config.output_step.as_deref(), Some("output.step"));
406	        assert_eq!(config.compare_step.as_deref(), Some("ref.step"));
407	        assert_eq!(config.stage, Stage(2, 3));
408	        assert!(config.verbose);
409	        // vertex_tolerance is in STL units (default mm), so 1e-6 mm
410	        assert!((config.vertex_tolerance_mm - 1e-6).abs() < 1e-15);
411	    }
412	
413	    #[test]
414	    fn tolerance_converted_from_stl_units() {
415	        // --stl-units=in means 1e-5 inches = 1e-5 * 25.4 mm
416	        let config = parse(&["input.stl", "--stl-units=in", "--vertex-tolerance=1e-5"]);
417	        let expected = 1e-5 * 25.4;
418	        assert!((config.vertex_tolerance_mm - expected).abs() < 1e-15);
419	    }
420	
421	    #[test]
422	    fn stage_at_least() {
423	        let s = Stage(2, 3);
424	        assert!(s.at_least(1, 1));
425	        assert!(s.at_least(2, 1));
426	        assert!(s.at_least(2, 3));
427	        assert!(!s.at_least(2, 4));
428	        assert!(!s.at_least(3, 1));
429	
430	        // Stage(2, 0) means all of stage 2 = (2, 9)
431	        let s2 = Stage(2, 0);
432	        assert!(s2.at_least(2, 6));
433	        assert!(s2.at_least(2, 9));
434	        assert!(!s2.at_least(3, 1));
435	
436	        // --stage=2.7 stops before the absorb (2.8) and boundary-refinement
437	        // (2.9) passes.
438	        let s27 = Stage(2, 7);
439	        assert!(!s27.at_least(2, 8));
440	    }
441	
442	    #[test]
443	    fn skip_stage_parses_and_matches() {
444	        let config = parse(&["input.stl", "--skip-stage=2.2,2.3,2.5,2.7,2.8,2.9"]);
445	        assert!(config.skip_stage(2, 2));
446	        assert!(config.skip_stage(2, 3));
447	        assert!(config.skip_stage(2, 5));
448	        assert!(config.skip_stage(2, 7));
449	        assert!(config.skip_stage(2, 8));
450	        assert!(config.skip_stage(2, 9));

55	/// Lower bound on cross product magnitude |n₁ × n₂| for a valid cylinder/sphere
56	/// seed pair. Since |n₁ × n₂| = sin(θ) for unit normals, this corresponds to a
57	/// minimum dihedral angle of arcsin(0.01) ≈ 0.57° (~0.6°). Seed pairs closer
58	/// than this are too nearly parallel for numerically stable axis estimation
59	/// (cylinders) or sphere fitting (spheres). Together with \`angular_tol\` (the
60	/// upper bound, typically 17.5°), this defines the valid seed-pair window
61	/// [~0.6°, angular_tol].
62	pub(crate) const MIN_CROSS_THRESHOLD: f64 = 0.01;
63	
64	/// Fast-reject multiplier for BFS vertex-distance checks. When a candidate face
65	/// has a vertex farther than \`REFIT_SKIP_MULTIPLIER × vertex_tol\` from the
66	/// current fitted surface, the distance is too large for a re-fit to absorb and
67	/// the face is rejected immediately without attempting re-fitting.
68	/// Set to 10.0 to allow aggressive refitting — the refit itself validates all
69	/// faces, so the only cost of a high multiplier is more refit attempts.
70	pub(crate) const REFIT_SKIP_MULTIPLIER: f64 = 10.0;
71	
72	/// Minimum number of mesh faces required to accept a cylindrical hypothesis.
73	/// Any real CAD tessellation of a cylinder produces at least 3 facets around
74	/// the circumference; this rejects spurious 2-face fits (e.g. adjacent torus
75	/// facets that locally approximate a cylinder).
76	pub(crate) const MIN_CYLINDER_FACES: usize = 3;
77	
78	/// Minimum number of mesh faces required to accept a spherical hypothesis.
79	/// Sphere fitting has 4 degrees of freedom (cx, cy, cz, r), so at least 4
80	/// non-degenerate faces are needed for a well-determined fit. This also rejects
81	/// spurious fits from small patches that are consistent with many surface types.
82	pub(crate) const MIN_SPHERE_FACES: usize = 4;
83	
84	/// Minimum number of mesh faces required to accept a conical hypothesis.
85	/// A cone has 5 degrees of freedom (apex xyz, axis direction 2, half-angle),
86	/// so at least 6 faces are needed for a well-determined and robust fit.
87	pub(crate) const MIN_CONE_FACES: usize = 6;
88	
89	/// Minimum number of mesh faces required to accept a toroidal hypothesis.
90	/// A torus has 7 degrees of freedom (center xyz, axis direction 2, R, r),
91	/// so at least 7 faces are needed for a well-determined fit.
92	pub(crate) const MIN_TORUS_FACES: usize = 7;
93	
94	/// Maximum sphere radius as a multiple of the mesh bounding-box diagonal.
95	/// With solid-angle coverage validation and surface-tolerance validation during
96	/// BFS, this no longer needs to be tight — those checks prevent pathological
97	/// growth. A large value serves as a numerical guardrail against degenerate fits.
98	pub(crate) const MAX_SPHERE_RADIUS_FACTOR: f64 = 1000.0;
99	
100	/// Minimum eigenvalue ratio for solid-angle coverage validation.
101	/// Centroid-to-center direction vectors of a genuine spherical hypothesis span
102	/// 3D, so all eigenvalues of their covariance matrix are substantial.
103	/// Fillet-strip growth produces nearly coplanar directions (λ₃ ≈ 0).
104	pub(crate) const MIN_SPHERE_EIGENVALUE_RATIO: f64 = 0.01;

1737	fn compute_seed_scores(mesh: &ConnectedMesh) -> (Vec<f64>, Vec<bool>) {
1738	    let n = mesh.faces.len();
1739	    let centroids: Vec<[f64; 3]> = mesh
1740	        .faces
1741	        .iter()
1742	        .map(|f| face_centroid(f, &mesh.vertices))
1743	        .collect();
1744	
1745	    // Step 1: per-face curvature summary.
1746	    let mut summary = vec![0.0_f64; n];
1747	    for fi in 0..n {
1748	        let Some(n1) = mesh.faces[fi].normal else { continue };
1749	        let vc = mesh.faces[fi].vertex_count as usize;
1750	        for &ni in &mesh.faces[fi].neighbors[..vc] {
1751	            if ni < 0 {
1752	                continue;
1753	            }
1754	            let ni = ni as usize;
1755	            let Some(n2) = mesh.faces[ni].normal else { continue };
1756	            let d = dot3(&n1, &n2).clamp(-1.0, 1.0);
1757	            let theta = d.acos();
1758	            let dx = centroids[ni][0] - centroids[fi][0];
1759	            let dy = centroids[ni][1] - centroids[fi][1];
1760	            let dz = centroids[ni][2] - centroids[fi][2];
1761	            let dist = (dx * dx + dy * dy + dz * dz).sqrt();
1762	            if dist > 1e-12 {
1763	                summary[fi] = summary[fi].max(theta / dist);
1764	            }
1765	        }
1766	    }
1767	
1768	    // Step 2: raw score = max |summary difference| vs neighbors.
1769	    let mut score = vec![0.0_f64; n];
1770	    for fi in 0..n {
1771	        let vc = mesh.faces[fi].vertex_count as usize;
1772	        for &ni in &mesh.faces[fi].neighbors[..vc] {
1773	            if ni >= 0 {
1774	                score[fi] = score[fi].max((summary[fi] - summary[ni as usize]).abs());
1775	            }
1776	        }
1777	    }
1778	
1779	    // Junction mask from the RAW score, before diffusion smears it. The
1780	    // criterion is LOCAL relative change: flag a face when its curvature
1781	    // changes by a large fraction of the local curvature magnitude. A
1782	    // mesh-global threshold fails here — sharp creases (κ jumps of several
1783	    // 1/mm) dominate the maximum and tangent fillet junctions (jumps of
1784	    // 1/r_fillet) fall under any fraction of it, yet tangent junctions are
1785	    // the primary target. Locally, a plane→fillet transition changes
1786	    // curvature by ~100% of the fillet curvature; interior faces of any
1787	    // uniform surface change by only tessellation noise.
1788	    let junction: Vec<bool> = (0..n)
1789	        .map(|fi| {
1790	            let vc = mesh.faces[fi].vertex_count as usize;
1791	            let mut local_scale = summary[fi];
1792	            for &ni in &mesh.faces[fi].neighbors[..vc] {
1793	                if ni >= 0 {
1794	                    local_scale = local_scale.max(summary[ni as usize]);
1795	                }
1796	            }
1797	            local_scale > 1e-12 && score[fi] > 0.5 * local_scale
1798	        })
1799	        .collect();
1800	
1801	    // Step 3: diffusion (Jacobi — deterministic).
1802	    for _pass in 0..3 {
1803	        let mut next = vec![0.0_f64; n];
1804	        for fi in 0..n {
1805	            let vc = mesh.faces[fi].vertex_count as usize;
1806	            let mut sum = 0.0_f64;
1807	            let mut count = 0_usize;
1808	            for &ni in &mesh.faces[fi].neighbors[..vc] {
1809	                if ni >= 0 {
1810	                    sum += score[ni as usize];
1811	                    count += 1;
1812	                }
1813	            }
1814	            next[fi] = if count > 0 {
1815	                0.5 * score[fi] + 0.5 * (sum / count as f64)
1816	            } else {
1817	                score[fi]
1818	            };
1819	        }
1820	        score = next;
1821	    }
1822	
1823	    (score, junction)
1824	}
1825

1835	        viz_level: config.viz_stages_level,

## 🤖 Assistant
<!-- msg_011Cd8FHCpSQ2RrLfhPkby8c -->

Sorry — I jumped ahead. I'd set up a task list and started read-only exploration (nothing modified yet). Here's the quick confirmation:

**Yes, it's a good and valid approach** — it's literally Rewrite Roadmap step 1 in the plan, and segmentation is well-suited to standalone testing precisely because it's fit-free: no classification, repair, or stage 3 needs to exist to judge it. From the exploration I did, three things make it cheap and one thing to know:

- **Validation is straightforward in one direction.** With `--compare`, check every boundary edge's midpoint against the reference STEP's edge set (`min_distance_to_shape` already exists and is parallelized) — that catches over-segmentation. Plus region-count comparison, where the expected answers already exist in the test suite's selection expectations (cube=6, block_with_hole=7, ball_on_cylinder=3, …). The reverse direction (did we *miss* a reference edge — under-segmentation) has one wrinkle: reference STEPs contain seam and degenerate-pole edges (cylinder seams, sphere poles) that legitimately have no mesh counterpart, so that check needs those filtered out or it'll report false misses. Region-count mismatches catch most under-segmentation anyway.
- **The viz is nearly free.** The `--viz-stages` plumbing, the render thread, and the overlay types all exist; boundary edges fit `LineOverlay` (arbitrary colored segments — sharp vs curvature-jump in different colors) and regions fit `FaceHighlight` with a color palette. `--viz-stages=2.1,2.2` would work with no new CLI surface.
- **It can coexist with the old pipeline during calibration.** New 2.1/2.2 run at the top of stage 2, `--stage=2.2` stops there; the old pipeline re-gates to ≥2.3 so `--stage=2` and the existing 494 tests keep working while we tune thresholds. (A few tests pin `--stage=2.1`/`2.2` with old meanings and would need small updates.)
- **One real design wrinkle** surfaced when I worked through the details: naive per-face curvature descriptors create *ghost boundaries* one facet row inside a plane that borders a tangent fillet — the plane's junction-row face inherits transition curvature from the junction edge itself, so it looks different from its plane neighbors. The fix is an iterative refinement: exclude already-classified boundary edges from the descriptors and reclassify to a fixpoint (ghosts un-fire once the junction edge is excluded). So the classifier is a bit more than the plan's two rules, but still simple, O(E), and deterministic.

Expect the corpus results to be clean on sharp-edged analytic models immediately, good-but-needing-calibration on tangent-fillet models (rounded cubes, stepped_block, nosecone), and informative on the coarse-fillet models (where 2-row fillets straddle the 25° sharp threshold — exactly the diagnostic data you'd want to see in the viz).

Want me to go ahead and implement it? The task list is already queued.

## 🤖 Assistant
<!-- msg_011Cd8FN89XyozyDkvNg8ojT -->

"The pipe-elbow strip problem" is this repo's canonical example of a correct surface that fit-driven growth can't discover because **no small seed neighborhood contains enough geometry to constrain the fit** — even though the surface fits the data perfectly.

**The setup.** `onshape_pipe_elbow` is a bent pipe: two straight cylindrical sections joined by a bend, and the bend is a torus patch (a circle swept along a 90° arc). CAD tessellators mesh swept surfaces anisotropically: the bend arrives as a stack of narrow quad **strips**, each strip a full ring around the tube (the minor circle, well sampled) but only one tessellation step along the sweep (the major arc, barely sampled). Real exporters do this for all swept/ruled surfaces — it's why the `tests/nurbs/` extrusion models deliberately ship as strip meshes too.

**Why local seeds fail.** A torus has 7 DOF, and the parameters that distinguish it from a cylinder — major radius R and axis — are only observable from how the tube *curves along the sweep*. A 1-ring seed sits essentially inside one strip: it spans maybe a degree of major arc. Over that span, the torus deviates from its osculating cylinder/cone by far less than vertex tolerance (the old plan noted "any adjacent pair of quad rows on a torus tube lies exactly on some cone"). So from any local seed:

- the **torus fit is ill-conditioned** — R and the axis are unconstrained by the data, the fit either fails or lands on garbage that doesn't validate; and
- the **cylinder fit succeeds** — the strip genuinely lies within tolerance of a cylinder.

BFS then grows the cylinder along the strip and *stalls at the strip boundary*: the next strip's vertices are off this strip's cylinder by more than vertex tolerance (the tube has visibly turned by then). Result: one hypothesis per strip. The model passed `--compare` with **290 cylindrical selections and 0 toroidal** — every strip individually within tolerance of the reference, which is why the old plan carried it as an accepted "nerfed" outcome for a while.

**The historical fixes** were all ways of manufacturing a bigger seed: first **strip-merge seeding** (commit 9555c8c) — when a 3-ring neighborhood spanned ≥2 distinct cylinder hypotheses, use it as a torus seed, because several strips together *do* span enough major arc to condition the fit — and later the hop-expanding direct toroidal seeds (up to 3 hops, targeting ≥16 faces) plus the toroidal-before-conical reordering, after which strip-merge became unreachable and was deleted (5691ea5). Note what these are: hand-tuned answers to "how big must a seed be to see the major curvature?", with the answer depending on tessellation density — i.e., exactly the kind of heuristic the redesign is trying to eliminate.

**Why I keep citing it in the architecture comparison:**

- The **seed-first inversion** (the contingency) doesn't fix it, because it changes *when* surface types compete but not *what the fitter sees*. From any elbow seed, the torus rung is still fed a 1-ring, still ill-conditioned, still loses; the cylinder rung wins its strip; the elbow decomposes into strips again. Rescuing it inside that design means re-adding escalation machinery ("when growth stalls, refit the stalled frontier at higher DOF using everything grown so far") — workable, but it's the strip-merge idea reborn.
- **Partition-then-classify** dissolves it structurally. The bend's interior edges are all smooth — dihedral steps are small, and the curvature descriptor varies *continuously* along the tube, so no curvature-jump boundary fires inside the bend. The whole bend is one region, and the ladder's torus rung fits it with vertices spanning the full 90° of major arc: R, axis, and r are all well-conditioned on the first try, with no seeding heuristics at all. The fit support is the maximal region rather than whatever a local neighborhood happens to sample — which is the general cure for anisotropic tessellation, not just for this model.

One adjacent caveat worth keeping in mind (distinct from the strip problem): the elbow's *junctions* — where the bend meets the straight pipes — are tangent tube-continuations, and their curvature jump is `σ_min`: 0 on the cylinder vs ~1/(R±r) on the torus, against a local scale of 1/r. That ratio is r/(R∓r), which for a typical elbow geometry hovers right around the J=0.5 threshold — so pipe_elbow is likely to be a calibration-interesting model for edge classification too, with the split repair as the backstop if the jump lands under threshold. The strips themselves, though, stop being a problem the moment the fit sees the whole region.

> The user's proposal is elegant—treat the cylinder as a limiting case of a torus where the major radius approaches infinity, allowing the fitter to start in that degenerate regime and smoothly transition to a finite-radius torus as curvature evidence accumulates. This is fundamentally a model promotion strategy where we begin with the simpler parameterization and let the data guide us toward the more complex one. It's worth examining seriously. With a cylinder-like torus (R very large), the second strip's vertices would deviate from the first strip's osculating cylinder by roughly R·Δφ²/2, where Δφ is the tessellation step size — so the question becomes whether this deviation falls within the tolerance thresholds that allow BFS expansion to continue across strips. The algorithm would skip the fast-reject multiplier and instead attempt a refit whenever a candidate face falls within the surface tolerance — in this case, the 0.15mm deviation is actually below the 0.4mm chordal tolerance, so it would qualify as a refit candidate. But there's a numerical problem: fitting a torus to just one strip leaves the model underdetermined, with a whole family of (R, axis, center) solutions equally valid, so the optimizer picks arbitrarily.
> 
> The fix is to reparameterize by curvature k = 1/R instead of radius, replacing the center with a point on the tube axis and sweep direction — this handles the degenerate case cleanly, since k=0 becomes a regular point (a cylinder), and small k values bend the spine into a constant-curvature arc. This curved-cylinder parameterization naturally extends to the infinite-radius limit without numerical blowup.
> 
> The model has seven degrees of freedom: three for position (two effective transverse), two for direction, one for tube radius, one for spine curvature k, and one for the bending plane azimuth — matching the torus's intrinsic DOF. During fitting, the algorithm starts with k≈0 (regularizing toward the simpler cylinder model), then as more data is included, k becomes identifiable and the Levenberg-Marquardt solver converges to the true radius; the fit expands around the bend and eventually settles on a genuine torus surface. At the end, model selection checks whether k times the spatial extent is negligible to decide if the simpler cylinder is sufficient.
> 
> This is the "model promotion during growth" I described earlier, but implemented more elegantly: rather than a discrete refit event when growth stalls, the model family continuously includes the degenerate case with numerically regular parameterizations at the limits. A single fitter handles the general model, and model reduction happens afterward — this is the classical approach in the literature for robust fitting (like circle-fitting that handles lines as a degenerate case).
> 
> The nested family structure is quite general: plane, cylinder, cone, torus, and sphere all fit within the surfaces of revolution framework, where sphere emerges as a torus limit with R→0. A universal parameterization like a general quadric-of-revolution with spine-arc and linearly-varying tube radius could serve as the growth model, though Dupin cyclides might offer an even cleaner universal family that encompasses all these cases. escue the seed-first approaches by fixing the strip problem: the model discovers curvature naturally as support accumulates, without relying on seed-size heuristics. The same mechanism handles sphere bands and cone cases if the generalization is broad enough—but here's the catch: a torus-only model would just tile a sphere with torus bands again, so the growth framework needs to include sphere as a degenerate case to truly unify the primitives, or it falls back into the same tiling problem.
> 
> The refit-trigger economics become the real constraint: relaxing the fast-reject threshold means many more expensive LM refits, so I need to be strategic about when to trigger them—perhaps using chordal tolerance or batching refits per region rather than attempting them everywhere.
> 
> Underdetermined parameters like k need regularization to keep the fitter from wandering; I'll bias it toward degenerate values (like k=0) with a ridge prior, though this adds tuning knobs like the regularization schedule as support grows.
> 
> There's also a risk that a more flexible growth model could absorb features it shouldn't—a cylinder-to-torus fit might bend itself across a tangent junction and misclassify frontier faces. systems with the full strip context, avoiding the underdetermined-phase problem where a partial fitter might reject valid faces. The general-model approach concentrates complexity into a single LM solver with regularization, which is harder to debug than the five specialized fitters already in brepper, though the infrastructure exists. Freeform surfaces still need a separate path since no analytic model captures B-splines, and a final model-reduction step would collapse degenerate cases rather than using the simplest-first ladder.
> 
> The user's parameterization-based conditioning is complementary to partition-first: it works well in the classification ladder where regions are already well-conditioned, and combined with seed-first as a contingency, it patches the elbow problem I identified—worth documenting in the plan's contingency section. Within partition-first itself, the split-repair path handles sub-regions where degeneracy still appears (like narrow strips), but it uses discrete simplest-first selection rather than continuous regularization. This connects to classical nested-model fitting—circle parameterized by curvature (lines as k=0), generalizing to Dupin cyclide fitting that covers all five primitives—I should mention these pointers briefly.

## 🤖 Assistant
<!-- msg_011Cd8FxaEP1Z8HdZbf6pHRV -->

Yes — that would work, and it's the principled version of what strip-merge seeding was gesturing at. What you're describing is the classical "nested model family" trick: fit with the more general model, parameterized so the degenerate case is a *regular interior point* of the parameter space, and let the data pull it off the degenerate limit as support accumulates. The circle-fitting literature does exactly this for lines (Pratt/Taubin parameterize circles by curvature k so a line is just k=0, not r=∞). For your case: parameterize the torus by a point on the tube spine, spine direction, tube radius r, spine *curvature* k = 1/R, and a bending-plane azimuth — at k=0 that's exactly a cylinder, and k is a well-behaved LM parameter with no infinity anywhere. The growth story becomes: fit starts at k≈0 on one strip, expansion reaches the next strip, the refit including it makes k identifiable, LM discovers the bend, and the surface sweeps around the elbow. One fitter, no discrete "promote" event, no seed-size heuristics.

Three implementation realities to go with it:

1. **The fast-reject multiplier is the actual blocker, not the fitter.** The reason cylinder BFS stalls today isn't just that the cylinder can't bend — it's that the neighbor strip's vertices sit ~R·Δφ²/2 off the current fit, which for elbow-scale geometry is hundreds to thousands of times vertex tolerance, far outside the 10× refit window. So `REFIT_SKIP_MULTIPLIER` gating must change for the general model — e.g. attempt refits for candidates within *surface* tolerance (chordal) of the current fit, or batch-refit per stalled frontier rather than per face. That's where the performance tuning would live, since refits over the whole region are the expensive operation.

2. **The underdetermined phase needs regularization.** While support is one strip, k is unidentifiable — LM will wander unless you bias it toward k=0 (ridge toward the degenerate model, relaxed as support grows). Fiddly but standard. Note the exactness assumption protects against the scarier failure: extra flexibility can't help the model absorb faces from a genuinely different surface, because within-vertex-tolerance is still the acceptance bar, and two distinct true surfaces agreeing to 5e-5 over an extended patch would be near-identical. Flexibility only expands which *true* shapes you can represent — which is the point.

3. **Torus-only generalization doesn't finish the job.** A torus-that-can't-become-a-sphere has the dual problem: on a sphere, the bent-tube model locks onto a latitude band, closes the ring, and stalls — spheres get tiled by torus bands, the old pathology in new clothes. The logical endpoint of your idea is growing with a model family that contains *all* the primitives as degenerate cases — which is exactly the "Unified SOR framework" already sketched in SURFACE_FITTING.md's algorithm reference (spine + profile: plane/cylinder/cone/sphere/torus as profile-curve special cases; the Dupin cyclide family is the mathematically complete version of this). Then classification becomes model *reduction* at the end — collapse k≈0 to cylinder, collapse to sphere when the spine degenerates — rather than a ladder of separate fitters. Freeform remains outside any such family, so the B-spline path stays a separate mechanism regardless.

Where this lands in the architecture decision: it's the missing piece that repairs the seed-first contingency's worst weakness. Seed-first + degenerate-limit growth model handles the elbow, sphere bands, and coarse fillets without seed-topology heuristics — which makes the contingency genuinely competitive rather than a downgrade. Partition-first doesn't need it, because fitting the maximal region gives you full-arc support up front and the five simple fitters stay simple; narrow-region degeneracy there is handled discretely (simplest-fit-first, merge repair). So I'd file your idea as: **the upgrade that belongs inside the contingency** (and the fallback tool if some future model class defeats edge classification), not a reason to change the primary plan.

Want me to fold this into the contingency section — the k=1/R parameterization, the refit-trigger change, the regularization need, and the torus-only caveat?

Done. The contingency section in [DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md](DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md) now includes a **"Required upgrade if adopted — degenerate-limit growth model"** block capturing your idea:

- The core mechanism: parameterize the torus by spine curvature `k = 1/R` (plus spine point/direction, tube radius, bending-plane azimuth) so `k = 0` *is* the cylinder — a regular point of the parameter space, Pratt/Taubin-style — and growth explores as a cylinder until admitting the next strip makes `k` identifiable.
- The three design consequences: the refit trigger is the real blocker (neighbor strips sit ~`R·Δφ²/2` off the fit, far outside the 10× `REFIT_SKIP_MULTIPLIER` window, so refits need chordal-proximity triggering, which is where the cost concentrates); the underdetermined phase needs ridge-toward-`k=0` regularization, with exactness bounding the over-flexibility risk; and a torus-only generalization re-creates the dual pathology on spheres, so the logical endpoint is the full SOR/Dupin-cyclide family with classification as model *reduction*.

I also updated the "Why it was not chosen" list to reference the upgrade — item 1 (small-seed conditioning) is now marked as repaired by it, and item 2 (freeform) explicitly notes the upgrade doesn't help there since no analytic family contains B-splines. The closing paragraph now names the growth model as part of what adoption would entail, and my project memory records the idea with your attribution.

docs: rewrite DEVELOPMENT_PLAN — stage 2 becomes partition-then-classify

Full redesign of the stage-2 architecture on paper (no code change; the
tree still runs the BFS pipeline until the Rewrite Roadmap lands). The
fit-driven BFS hypothesis pipeline had accumulated heuristics-on-heuristics
— stage ordering, seeding masks, coverage acceptance tests, greedy
selection, absorb, refinement — each compensating for greedy commitment
and accidental sub-region exact fits. The new design attacks both causes
directly:

- Design Principles: exactness makes validation decisive; tessellators
  emit seam edges along B-rep boundaries even at tangencies (verified on
  the nosecone 2026-07-17), so the true segmentation is a partition of
  the face graph whose boundaries are mesh edges; fit only maximal
  regions so accidental sub-region fits are never hypothesized; repair by
  validation, locally; invariants (coverage, connectivity, per-region
  validity, determinism) enforced by assertion.
- New stage 2: 2.1 fit-free edge classification (sharp dihedral OR
  signed two-sided curvature-jump descriptor sigma_max/sigma_min, local-
  relative J=0.5 + noise floor; two-sided catches the capsule's equal-
  radius sphere/cylinder tangency via sigma_min), 2.2 connected-region
  extraction, 2.3 classification ladder in ascending DOF (plane, sphere,
  cylinder, cone, torus, freeform — first fit validating all vertices at
  vertex tolerance wins; per-facet planes are the terminal fallback, so
  planar-only mode becomes --surface-types=planar with no absorb slop),
  2.4 repair loop (merge = the tight absorb rule reborn at vertex
  tolerance; split = re-run 2.1 at lowered thresholds inside failing
  regions, preferring all-analytic splits before freeform; boundary
  relabel = old 2.9 promoted, with connectivity re-split), 2.5 unchanged
  SelectedSurface output for stage 3.
- Stages 1/3/4 carry over as as-built docs with cross-references fixed;
  the old 2.7 NURBS fitting machinery survives intact as the freeform
  rung (its region-gathering front end is superseded by the partition).
- Abandoned Approaches section: per-type BFS generation, stage ordering,
  seed ordering/junction/large_sphere masks, seed topologies, the four
  coverage/consistency acceptance tests, greedy area selection, absorb
  (tight rule's targets cannot form; loose rule already condemned by
  measurement), per-face multi-hypothesis fields, NURBS gathering — each
  with the measured evidence for why it existed and why it is now
  structurally unnecessary.
- Contingency section (considered, not chosen): seed-first multi-type
  competition — invert the old loops, all surface types compete from the
  same seed, largest-area-with-DOF-tiebreak wins; sound via the
  sub-region lemma but keeps small-seed conditioning and freeform
  problems. Its required upgrade if ever adopted: the degenerate-limit
  growth model — parameterize the torus by spine curvature k=1/R so k=0
  IS the cylinder (Pratt/Taubin-style), grow at k~0 with ridge
  regularization until the data identifies the bend; notes that the 10x
  REFIT_SKIP_MULTIPLIER window is the real pipe-elbow strip blocker and
  that a torus-only generalization would re-tile spheres with bands (the
  endpoint is the full SOR/Dupin-cyclide family with classification as
  model reduction).
- Rewrite Roadmap: one-shot transition — no reconciliation of old and new
  stage numbers; when 2.1/2.2 land the new numbering owns --stage and
  --viz-stages and the old BFS pipeline is nerfed in place (disconnected
  from stage2(), deleted in a later pass); suite red until 2.3-2.5 land,
  models re-enabled easiest first. Step 1 records the verified viz plan:
  boundary edges as class-colored LineOverlay segments, regions as
  per-region FaceHighlight with a deterministic palette; EdgeHighlight is
  NOT the right primitive (outlines whole faces) and stays TODO.md's
  delete candidate. Step 1's threshold calibration is the decision gate
  for falling back to the contingency.

---

docs: rewrite DEVELOPMENT_PLAN — stage 2 becomes partition-then-classify

Full redesign of the stage-2 architecture on paper (no code change; the
tree still runs the BFS pipeline until the Rewrite Roadmap lands). The
fit-driven BFS hypothesis pipeline had accumulated heuristics-on-heuristics
— stage ordering, seeding masks, coverage acceptance tests, greedy
selection, absorb, refinement — each compensating for greedy commitment
and accidental sub-region exact fits. The new design attacks both causes
directly:

- Design Principles: exactness makes validation decisive; tessellators
  emit seam edges along B-rep boundaries even at tangencies (verified on
  the nosecone 2026-07-17), so the true segmentation is a partition of
  the face graph whose boundaries are mesh edges; fit only maximal
  regions so accidental sub-region fits are never hypothesized; repair by
  validation, locally; invariants (coverage, connectivity, per-region
  validity, determinism) enforced by assertion.
- New stage 2: 2.1 fit-free edge classification (sharp dihedral OR
  signed two-sided curvature-jump descriptor sigma_max/sigma_min, local-
  relative J=0.5 + noise floor; two-sided catches the capsule's equal-
  radius sphere/cylinder tangency via sigma_min), 2.2 connected-region
  extraction, 2.3 classification ladder in ascending DOF (plane, sphere,
  cylinder, cone, torus, freeform — first fit validating all vertices at
  vertex tolerance wins; per-facet planes are the terminal fallback, so
  planar-only mode becomes --surface-types=planar with no absorb slop),
  2.4 repair loop (merge = the tight absorb rule reborn at vertex
  tolerance; split = re-run 2.1 at lowered thresholds inside failing
  regions, preferring all-analytic splits before freeform; boundary
  relabel = old 2.9 promoted, with connectivity re-split), 2.5 unchanged
  SelectedSurface output for stage 3.
- Stages 1/3/4 carry over as as-built docs with cross-references fixed;
  the old 2.7 NURBS fitting machinery survives intact as the freeform
  rung (its region-gathering front end is superseded by the partition).
- Abandoned Approaches section: per-type BFS generation, stage ordering,
  seed ordering/junction/large_sphere masks, seed topologies, the four
  coverage/consistency acceptance tests, greedy area selection, absorb
  (tight rule's targets cannot form; loose rule already condemned by
  measurement), per-face multi-hypothesis fields, NURBS gathering — each
  with the measured evidence for why it existed and why it is now
  structurally unnecessary.
- Contingency section (considered, not chosen): seed-first multi-type
  competition — invert the old loops, all surface types compete from the
  same seed, largest-area-with-DOF-tiebreak wins; sound via the
  sub-region lemma but keeps small-seed conditioning and freeform
  problems. Its required upgrade if ever adopted: the degenerate-limit
  growth model — parameterize the torus by spine curvature k=1/R so k=0
  IS the cylinder (Pratt/Taubin-style), grow at k~0 with ridge
  regularization until the data identifies the bend; notes that the 10x
  REFIT_SKIP_MULTIPLIER window is the real pipe-elbow strip blocker and
  that a torus-only generalization would re-tile spheres with bands (the
  endpoint is the full SOR/Dupin-cyclide family with classification as
  model reduction).
- Rewrite Roadmap: one-shot transition — no reconciliation of old and new
  stage numbers; when 2.1/2.2 land the new numbering owns --stage and
  --viz-stages and the old BFS pipeline is nerfed in place (disconnected
  from stage2(), deleted in a later pass); suite red until 2.3-2.5 land,
  models re-enabled easiest first. Step 1 records the verified viz plan:
  boundary edges as class-colored LineOverlay segments, regions as
  per-region FaceHighlight with a deterministic palette; EdgeHighlight is
  NOT the right primitive (outlines whole faces) and stays TODO.md's
  delete candidate. Step 1's threshold calibration is the decision gate
  for falling back to the contingency.

---
Generated with assistance from Claude Fable 5 (Anthropic, 2026)

User prompt(s):
- "I think development of this project has gotten to the point where we
  keep adding heuristics to make specific models pass, but really we
  should be focusing on improving the core surface fitting and BFS
  approach. Think hard about how the algorithms could be restructured...
  Give me some options for how to update DEVELOPMENT_PLAN for a
  back-to-basics clean-up."
- "Rewrite DEVELOPMENT_PLAN to implement Option 1. Don't gate new
  functionality behind a flag or attempt an incremental rewrite - we'll
  jump to the new structure immediately and then incrementally work on
  making more and more test models pass. For existing functionality that
  isn't part of the plan but could potentially be useful in the future,
  move it to an 'Abandoned Approaches' section..." (Option 1 =
  segmentation-first partition-then-classify, from the options presented)
- "Contrast the approach you just documented to a different approach:
  ... invert the loops: iterate over possible seed faces ... then for
  each seed face, iterating over surface types ... Select the largest
  surface type, and continue." / "Sure, add the contingency section."
- "It seems like this could be solved by letting the torus fitter
  effectively fit infinite-radius torus while in the early stages of
  expansion - until the fitter starts discovering the curvature." / "yes"
  (re the pipe-elbow strip problem; folded into the contingency as the
  degenerate-limit growth model)
- "Now update the roadmap with the viz details. Keep in mind that we're
  going to one-shot the transition to the new approach, so you don't
  need to reconcile old stage numbers with new stage numbers. You can
  simply nerf the old stage code until you reimplement it with the new
  stage code. Then commit."

The old pipeline functions become dead code once 2.1/2.2 take over, which will trigger clippy warnings unless I suppress them—either with `#[allow(dead_code)]` annotations on the nerfed modules or by keeping a hidden reference to them in tests. The cleanest approach is marking the old modules at the top level to silence the warnings while the code stays in-tree as reference material. I'll need to add `#[allow(dead_code)]` attributes to private functions like `select_surfaces` that are no longer called from the public API. The test suite will fail broadly for integration tests covering stages 2-4, which is expected during this refactor, but unit tests and stage 1 integration tests should still pass since the core fitter logic is unchanged. I should compile and address warnings as they appear rather than trying to preemptively fix all dead code issues. Integration tests should keep compiling since I'm preserving the data structures. For Stage2Output, I need to add the new fields for edge classification and regions, which means refactoring the literal constructions in stage2() — there are several early returns that build Stage2Output directly, so I'll consolidate those into a single construction point. The main pipeline in brepper.rs chains stage1 through stage4 based on config, and with the nerf in place, stage2 will return empty selected_surfaces by default.
> 
> The tricky part is handling the transition gracefully: if someone runs with --stage=4.1 on default settings, stage3 would operate on empty selections and likely crash or produce garbage. Since stages 3 and 4 stay red, the cleanest approach is to have stage2 detect when it's at the frontier and print a note to the user, then let main check whether to proceed to stage3 or stop early.
> 
> I'm now looking at the edges module to understand the edge classification logic. Now I'm confirming the neighbor indexing from the merge code—specifically how neighbors are assigned when faces are merged, and then working through the algorithm to precompute per-edge properties like signed theta and curvature. The key challenge is finding the back-slot efficiently: for each face pair, I need to locate which edge slot in the neighbor face points back to the original face, which requires scanning through the neighbor's edge list. There's an edge case with degenerate meshes where two faces might share multiple edges, but the stage 1 validation probably filters those out; if not, I'll just take the first match when scanning.
> 
> For the sign convention, I'm verifying that the curvature sign is symmetric across both sides of an edge—computing it per half-edge directly using the normal and edge vector difference should give consistent results without needing explicit face pairing, since the formula itself is symmetric in the vertex normals and positions.
> 
> Now I'm moving into the classification logic: sharp edges are identified by angular threshold, and for jump classification I'm computing the maximum and minimum curvatures across manifold half-edges for each face, then comparing these descriptors between adjacent faces. κ_j is small compared to the cross-tube curvature 1/r, so the jump between the plane and fillet faces is approximately 1/r scaled by the width ratio. The fillet face edges include the junction edge, cross-tube, and along-tube contributions, with the minimum curvature staying near zero from the along-tube direction.
> 
> The condition fires when the jump exceeds 0.5 times the scale (1/r), which happens in typical cases where plane facets are wider than fillet rows. This handles the first iteration of cleanup, and subsequent iterations refine exclusions for edge cases where the fillet is wider than the plane. I'm also accounting for degenerate faces with no normal by skipping them, and calculating a floor threshold based on vertex tolerance divided by the squared edge length—with typical values this gives a very small radius threshold that works well for fine meshes.
> 
> Now I'm moving on to implement region detection and boundary accessors. I'm using union-find to group connected interior faces together, then collecting the members of each region and sorting them deterministically by minimum face index. The output structure will include a vector of regions, each containing its member faces and unique vertices.
> 
> Adding region data structures... Completing stage 2.2 by extracting regions from the classified edges and building the output structure, then noting that stage 2.3 and beyond aren't implemented yet.
> 
> The challenge is that the old pipeline code constructs Stage2Output with the original field set, but now there are three additional fields to populate — so I need to either update the old code to fill those new fields or refactor it differently. I could mark the old implementation as dead code and write a fresh stage2 function, but that means the old body won't compile as-is since it's missing the new Stage2Output fields. I'm realizing a cleaner approach: group the new fields into a single `Segmentation` struct with `Default` implemented, then the old code just needs one line per literal setting `segmentation: Segmentation::default()` instead of adding three separate fields everywhere.
> 
> Now I'm thinking about where to build the visualization overlays for the segmentation — I'll add functions in mod.rs that create line and face highlight overlays, using red for sharp edges and orange for jump edges.  For regions, I'll assign colors using a golden-ratio hue distribution with saturation and value tuned for visibility, then render them with full opacity over the mesh.  I need a small HSV-to-RGB conversion helper, and the viz flow will handle user actions like Quit (returning early with partial output) or NextStep/NextSeed (continuing the loop). The status text will display the current stage with counts of sharp edges, jump discontinuities, and interior edges across rounds. I'll run the binary on each model to observe the actual region counts before writing assertions, since the cylinder wall behavior depends on tessellation resolution and I don't want to guess wrong. > The main binary in brepper.rs calls stage2 with the same signature, so that's fine. For visualization, I'm converting vertex positions from f64 to f32 arrays, and the LineOverlay renderer handles position pairs well even for large meshes with tens of thousands of segments.
>
