//! Stage 2.1: fit-free edge classification.
//!
//! Labels every manifold mesh edge INTERIOR or BOUNDARY using two fit-free
//! signals (DEVELOPMENT_PLAN §2.1): a sharp dihedral angle, or a jump in the
//! per-face two-sided discrete-curvature descriptor (σ_max, σ_min) across
//! the edge. The true segmentation's boundaries are a subset of mesh edges
//! (tessellators emit seam edges along B-rep face boundaries, even at
//! tangencies), so this classification IS the segmentation; stage 2.2
//! extracts its connected regions and stage 2.4 (future) repairs mistakes.
//!
//! The curvature-jump rule runs iteratively: descriptors are computed over a
//! face's NON-boundary edges only, then edges are (re)classified, until the
//! classification is stable. The iteration exists to kill "ghost"
//! boundaries: the plane face bordering a tangent fillet inherits the
//! junction edge's transition curvature into its descriptor, which on round
//! one makes it differ from its plane neighbors one row in. Once the
//! junction edge is classified boundary it leaves the descriptor, the
//! plane face's descriptor returns to ≈0, and the ghost un-fires (the
//! junction edge itself keeps firing — the fillet side's descriptor is
//! supported by its interior cross-tube edges).

use super::{dot3, face_centroid};
use crate::stage1::ConnectedMesh;

/// Local-relative curvature-jump threshold J (DEVELOPMENT_PLAN §2.1): an
/// edge is a boundary when the descriptor jump exceeds this fraction of the
/// local curvature magnitude. Carried over from the junction-mask finding:
/// thresholds relative to a mesh-global maximum fail because sharp creases
/// dominate it while tangent junctions fall below any global fraction.
pub const JUMP_RATIO: f64 = 0.5;

/// Noise-floor coefficient: κ_floor = FLOOR_COEFF · vertex_tolerance / d²
/// per edge (centroid distance d). Vertex position noise ε perturbs the
/// dihedral by ~ε/d and the discrete curvature by ~ε/d²; the floor keeps
/// exact-coplanar plane interiors (κ ≡ 0 ± noise) from ever firing the
/// relative test. Calibration constant — revisit against the corpus.
pub const FLOOR_COEFF: f64 = 4.0;

/// Cap on classify/re-descriptor rounds. Convergence is typically 2–3
/// rounds (fire junctions, ghosts un-fire); the cap guards oscillation.
pub const MAX_ROUNDS: usize = 10;

/// Classification of one manifold mesh edge.
#[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy, PartialEq, Eq, Default)]
pub enum EdgeClass {
    /// Both faces on the same surface region.
    #[default]
    Interior,
    /// Dihedral angle exceeds the angular tolerance — a hard feature edge.
    Sharp,
    /// G1-smooth but G2-discontinuous — a tangent junction found by the
    /// curvature-jump rule.
    Jump,
}

/// Stage 2.1 output: per-half-edge classes plus the curvature evidence,
/// kept for the 2.4 split repair (re-run at lowered thresholds), for
/// `--compare` diagnostics, and for viz.
///
/// All per-edge arrays are indexed `[face][slot]`, parallel to
/// `MeshFace.neighbors`: slot `e` is the edge from `vertex_indices[e]` to
/// `vertex_indices[(e+1) % vertex_count]`. Values are mirrored — the two
/// half-edges of a manifold edge always carry identical class/κ (computed
/// from symmetric formulas, not copied).
#[derive(Debug, Default)]
pub struct EdgeClassification {
    /// Class per half-edge. Slots without a manifold neighbor stay Interior
    /// (they never participate in region unions anyway).
    pub class: Vec<[EdgeClass; 4]>,
    /// Signed discrete normal curvature κ = ±θ/d per half-edge (positive
    /// convex, negative concave); NaN where no manifold neighbor.
    pub kappa: Vec<[f64; 4]>,
    /// Final per-face curvature descriptor: max signed κ over the face's
    /// non-boundary manifold edges (0.0 when it has none).
    pub sigma_max: Vec<f64>,
    /// Final per-face descriptor minimum (see `sigma_max`).
    pub sigma_min: Vec<f64>,
    /// Rounds until the jump classification stabilized (or MAX_ROUNDS).
    pub rounds: usize,
    /// Undirected manifold-edge counts by final class.
    pub sharp_count: usize,
    pub jump_count: usize,
    pub interior_count: usize,
}

impl EdgeClassification {
    /// Is the manifold edge across `faces[fi]`'s slot `e` a region boundary?
    pub fn is_boundary(&self, fi: usize, e: usize) -> bool {
        self.class[fi][e] != EdgeClass::Interior
    }
}

/// Classify every manifold mesh edge (stage 2.1). Deterministic, O(E) per
/// round with a small bounded round count.
pub fn classify_edges(
    mesh: &ConnectedMesh,
    vertex_tolerance: f64,
    angular_tolerance: f64,
) -> EdgeClassification {
    let n = mesh.faces.len();
    let centroids: Vec<[f64; 3]> = mesh
        .faces
        .iter()
        .map(|f| face_centroid(f, &mesh.vertices))
        .collect();

    // Per half-edge: signed κ, sharpness, and the noise floor. Each side is
    // computed independently from symmetric formulas, so mirrored slots get
    // identical values without explicit pairing.
    let mut kappa = vec![[f64::NAN; 4]; n];
    let mut sharp = vec![[false; 4]; n];
    let mut floor = vec![[f64::INFINITY; 4]; n];
    for fi in 0..n {
        let Some(n1) = mesh.faces[fi].normal else { continue };
        let vc = mesh.faces[fi].vertex_count as usize;
        for e in 0..vc {
            let ni = mesh.faces[fi].neighbors[e];
            if ni < 0 {
                continue;
            }
            let ni = ni as usize;
            let Some(n2) = mesh.faces[ni].normal else { continue };
            let theta = dot3(&n1, &n2).clamp(-1.0, 1.0).acos();
            let dx = centroids[ni][0] - centroids[fi][0];
            let dy = centroids[ni][1] - centroids[fi][1];
            let dz = centroids[ni][2] - centroids[fi][2];
            let d = (dx * dx + dy * dy + dz * dz).sqrt();
            if d <= 1e-12 {
                continue;
            }
            // Sign: convex (+) when the neighbor's centroid lies below this
            // face's plane. Symmetric — for a convex edge each centroid is
            // below the other face's plane.
            let below = n1[0] * dx + n1[1] * dy + n1[2] * dz <= 0.0;
            kappa[fi][e] = if below { theta / d } else { -theta / d };
            sharp[fi][e] = theta > angular_tolerance;
            floor[fi][e] = FLOOR_COEFF * vertex_tolerance / (d * d);
        }
    }

    // Iterate: descriptors over non-boundary edges → (re)classify jumps.
    let mut jump = vec![[false; 4]; n];
    let mut sigma_max = vec![0.0_f64; n];
    let mut sigma_min = vec![0.0_f64; n];
    let mut rounds = 0;
    for _round in 0..MAX_ROUNDS {
        rounds += 1;
        for fi in 0..n {
            let vc = mesh.faces[fi].vertex_count as usize;
            let mut smax = f64::NEG_INFINITY;
            let mut smin = f64::INFINITY;
            for e in 0..vc {
                let k = kappa[fi][e];
                if k.is_finite() && !sharp[fi][e] && !jump[fi][e] {
                    smax = smax.max(k);
                    smin = smin.min(k);
                }
            }
            if smax == f64::NEG_INFINITY {
                smax = 0.0;
                smin = 0.0;
            }
            sigma_max[fi] = smax;
            sigma_min[fi] = smin;
        }

        let mut changed = false;
        let mut new_jump = vec![[false; 4]; n];
        for fi in 0..n {
            let vc = mesh.faces[fi].vertex_count as usize;
            for e in 0..vc {
                if !kappa[fi][e].is_finite() || sharp[fi][e] {
                    continue;
                }
                let ni = mesh.faces[fi].neighbors[e] as usize;
                let jmp = (sigma_max[fi] - sigma_max[ni])
                    .abs()
                    .max((sigma_min[fi] - sigma_min[ni]).abs());
                let scale = sigma_max[fi]
                    .abs()
                    .max(sigma_min[fi].abs())
                    .max(sigma_max[ni].abs())
                    .max(sigma_min[ni].abs());
                let fire = jmp > JUMP_RATIO * scale && jmp > floor[fi][e];
                new_jump[fi][e] = fire;
                if fire != jump[fi][e] {
                    changed = true;
                }
            }
        }
        jump = new_jump;
        if !changed {
            break;
        }
    }

    // Final classes + undirected counts.
    let mut class = vec![[EdgeClass::Interior; 4]; n];
    let (mut sharp_count, mut jump_count, mut interior_count) = (0, 0, 0);
    for fi in 0..n {
        let vc = mesh.faces[fi].vertex_count as usize;
        for e in 0..vc {
            let ni = mesh.faces[fi].neighbors[e];
            if ni < 0 || !kappa[fi][e].is_finite() {
                continue;
            }
            class[fi][e] = if sharp[fi][e] {
                EdgeClass::Sharp
            } else if jump[fi][e] {
                EdgeClass::Jump
            } else {
                EdgeClass::Interior
            };
            if fi < ni as usize {
                match class[fi][e] {
                    EdgeClass::Sharp => sharp_count += 1,
                    EdgeClass::Jump => jump_count += 1,
                    EdgeClass::Interior => interior_count += 1,
                }
            }
        }
    }

    EdgeClassification {
        class,
        kappa,
        sigma_max,
        sigma_min,
        rounds,
        sharp_count,
        jump_count,
        interior_count,
    }
}

```
//! Stage 2.2: region extraction — connected components of the face graph
//! over INTERIOR edges (union-find). Regions are maximal, mesh-connected by
//! construction, and every face belongs to exactly one region (a single
//! facet is a valid region — the terminal per-facet-plane fallback of the
//! classification ladder depends on that). Region numbering is
//! deterministic: regions are ordered by their smallest member face index.

use super::edges::EdgeClassification;
use crate::stage1::ConnectedMesh;

/// One region of the partition: a maximal set of faces connected across
/// INTERIOR edges.
#[derive(Debug, Default)]
pub struct Region {
    /// Member mesh face indices, ascending.
    pub faces: Vec<usize>,
    /// Member mesh vertex indices (union of face vertices), sorted, deduped.
    pub vertices: Vec<usize>,
}

fn find(parent: &mut [usize], mut x: usize) -> usize {
    while parent[x] != x {
        parent[x] = parent[parent[x]]; // path halving
        x = parent[x];
    }
    x
}

/// Extract regions from the edge classification. Returns
/// `(face_region, regions)` where `face_region[fi]` is the region id of
/// face `fi`.
pub fn extract_regions(
    mesh: &ConnectedMesh,
    edge_classes: &EdgeClassification,
) -> (Vec<usize>, Vec<Region>) {
    let n = mesh.faces.len();
    let mut parent: Vec<usize> = (0..n).collect();

    for fi in 0..n {
        let vc = mesh.faces[fi].vertex_count as usize;
        for e in 0..vc {
            let ni = mesh.faces[fi].neighbors[e];
            if ni >= 0 && !edge_classes.is_boundary(fi, e) {
                let ra = find(&mut parent, fi);
                let rb = find(&mut parent, ni as usize);
                if ra != rb {
                    // Union by smaller root index keeps numbering stable.
                    parent[ra.max(rb)] = ra.min(rb);
                }
            }
        }
    }

    let mut face_region = vec![usize::MAX; n];
    let mut region_of_root = vec![usize::MAX; n];
    let mut regions: Vec<Region> = Vec::new();
    for fi in 0..n {
        let root = find(&mut parent, fi);
        let id = if region_of_root[root] == usize::MAX {
            regions.push(Region::default());
            region_of_root[root] = regions.len() - 1;
            regions.len() - 1
        } else {
            region_of_root[root]
        };
        face_region[fi] = id;
        regions[id].faces.push(fi);
    }

    for region in &mut regions {
        let mut vertices: Vec<usize> = region
            .faces
            .iter()
            .flat_map(|&fi| {
                let vc = mesh.faces[fi].vertex_count as usize;
                mesh.faces[fi].vertex_indices[..vc].iter().copied()
            })
            .collect();
        vertices.sort_unstable();
        vertices.dedup();
        region.vertices = vertices;
    }

    (face_region, regions)
}

```

```
// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
// Stage 2 entry: partition-then-classify (rewrite frontier = 2.2)
// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

pub fn stage2(
    config: &Config,
    mesh: ConnectedMesh,
    viz: Option<&crate::viz::VizSender>,
) -> Result<Stage2Output, Stage2Error> {
    // --- Stage 2.1: edge classification (fit-free) ---
    let t = Instant::now();
    let edge_classes = edges::classify_edges(
        &mesh,
        config.vertex_tolerance_mm,
        config.angular_tolerance_rad,
    );
    if config.verbosity >= 1 {
        let total =
            edge_classes.sharp_count + edge_classes.jump_count + edge_classes.interior_count;
        eprintln!(
            "Stage 2.1 ({:.3}s): Edge classification: {} manifold edges — {} sharp, {} curvature-jump, {} interior ({} round{})",
            t.elapsed().as_secs_f64(),
            total,
            edge_classes.sharp_count,
            edge_classes.jump_count,
            edge_classes.interior_count,
            edge_classes.rounds,
            if edge_classes.rounds == 1 { "" } else { "s" },
        );
    }
    let mut segmentation = Segmentation {
        edge_classes,
        face_region: Vec::new(),
        regions: Vec::new(),
    };
    if config.viz_active(2, 1) {
        if let Some(vs) = viz {
            let overlay = segmentation_edges_overlay(&mesh, &segmentation.edge_classes);
            if matches!(vs.show_and_wait(overlay), VizAction::Quit) {
                return Ok(segmentation_only_output(mesh, segmentation));
            }
        }
    }
    if !config.stage.at_least(2, 2) {
        return Ok(segmentation_only_output(mesh, segmentation));
    }

    // --- Stage 2.2: region extraction ---
    let t = Instant::now();
    let (face_region, region_list) =
        regions::extract_regions(&mesh, &segmentation.edge_classes);
    if config.verbosity >= 1 {
        let single = region_list.iter().filter(|r| r.faces.len() == 1).count();
        let largest = region_list.iter().map(|r| r.faces.len()).max().unwrap_or(0);
        eprintln!(
            "Stage 2.2 ({:.3}s): Region extraction: {} regions over {} faces ({} single-face, largest {} faces)",
            t.elapsed().as_secs_f64(),
            region_list.len(),
            mesh.faces.len(),
            single,
            largest,
        );
        if config.verbosity >= 2 {
            for (i, r) in region_list.iter().enumerate() {
                if r.faces.len() > 1 {
                    eprintln!(
                        "  Region {}: {} faces, {} vertices (first face {})",
                        i,
                        r.faces.len(),
                        r.vertices.len(),
                        r.faces[0],
                    );
                }
            }
        }
    }
    segmentation.face_region = face_region;
    segmentation.regions = region_list;
    if config.viz_active(2, 2) {
        if let Some(vs) = viz {
            let overlay = segmentation_regions_overlay(&mesh, &segmentation);
            if matches!(vs.show_and_wait(overlay), VizAction::Quit) {
                return Ok(segmentation_only_output(mesh, segmentation));
            }
        }
    }

    // --- Rewrite frontier: 2.3 classification / 2.4 repair / 2.5 selection ---
    if config.stage.at_least(2, 3) && !config.quiet {
        eprintln!(
            "Stage 2.3+: not yet implemented (partition-then-classify rewrite in progress) — stopping after stage 2.2"
        );
    }
    Ok(segmentation_only_output(mesh, segmentation))
}

/// Stage2Output for the current rewrite frontier: segmentation only, no
/// hypotheses or selected surfaces (stages 3/4 are unreachable until the
/// classification ladder lands).
fn segmentation_only_output(mesh: ConnectedMesh, segmentation: Segmentation) -> Stage2Output {
    Stage2Output {
        mesh,
        planar_hypotheses: Vec::new(),
        conical_hypotheses: Vec::new(),
        spherical_hypotheses: Vec::new(),
        toroidal_hypotheses: Vec::new(),
        bspline_hypotheses: Vec::new(),
        selected_surfaces: Vec::new(),
        segmentation,
    }
}

// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
// Segmentation viz (--viz-stages=2.1 / 2.2)
// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

const SHARP_EDGE_COLOR: [f32; 4] = [0.9, 0.1, 0.1, 1.0];
const JUMP_EDGE_COLOR: [f32; 4] = [1.0, 0.6, 0.0, 1.0];

/// Boundary-edge line segments for viz, one segment per undirected boundary
/// edge, offset slightly along the adjacent faces' average normal so they
/// sit visibly on top of the rendered mesh instead of z-fighting it.
/// Returns (sharp_positions, jump_positions) as [p0, p1, p0, p1, ...] pairs.
fn boundary_line_positions(
    mesh: &ConnectedMesh,
    ec: &EdgeClassification,
) -> (Vec<[f32; 3]>, Vec<[f32; 3]>) {
    let mut min = [f64::INFINITY; 3];
    let mut max = [f64::NEG_INFINITY; 3];
    for v in &mesh.vertices {
        for (k, c) in [v.x, v.y, v.z].into_iter().enumerate() {
            min[k] = min[k].min(c);
            max[k] = max[k].max(c);
        }
    }
    let diag = ((max[0] - min[0]).powi(2)
        + (max[1] - min[1]).powi(2)
        + (max[2] - min[2]).powi(2))
    .sqrt();
    let offset = if diag.is_finite() { diag * 5e-4 } else { 0.0 };

    let mut sharp = Vec::new();
    let mut jump = Vec::new();
    for fi in 0..mesh.faces.len() {
        let vc = mesh.faces[fi].vertex_count as usize;
        for e in 0..vc {
            let ni = mesh.faces[fi].neighbors[e];
            if ni < fi as i32 {
                continue; // open edge, or emitted from the other side
            }
            let class = ec.class[fi][e];
            if class == EdgeClass::Interior {
                continue;
            }
            let n1 = mesh.faces[fi].normal.unwrap_or([0.0; 3]);
            let n2 = mesh.faces[ni as usize].normal.unwrap_or(n1);
            let mut nn = [n1[0] + n2[0], n1[1] + n2[1], n1[2] + n2[2]];
            let len = (nn[0] * nn[0] + nn[1] * nn[1] + nn[2] * nn[2]).sqrt();
            if len > 1e-12 {
                for c in &mut nn {
                    *c /= len;
                }
            } else {
                nn = [0.0; 3];
            }
            let va = &mesh.vertices[mesh.faces[fi].vertex_indices[e]];
            let vb = &mesh.vertices[mesh.faces[fi].vertex_indices[(e + 1) % vc]];
            let pa = [
                (va.x + nn[0] * offset) as f32,
                (va.y + nn[1] * offset) as f32,
                (va.z + nn[2] * offset) as f32,
            ];
            let pb = [
                (vb.x + nn[0] * offset) as f32,
                (vb.y + nn[1] * offset) as f32,
                (vb.z + nn[2] * offset) as f32,
            ];
            let out = if class == EdgeClass::Sharp {
                &mut sharp
            } else {
                &mut jump
            };
            out.push(pa);
            out.push(pb);
        }
    }
    (sharp, jump)
}

fn push_boundary_lines(overlay: &mut viz::VizOverlay, mesh: &ConnectedMesh, ec: &EdgeClassification) {
    let (sharp, jump) = boundary_line_positions(mesh, ec);
    if !sharp.is_empty() {
        overlay.lines.push(viz::LineOverlay {
            positions: sharp,
            color: SHARP_EDGE_COLOR,
            no_depth_test: false,
        });
    }
    if !jump.is_empty() {
        overlay.lines.push(viz::LineOverlay {
            positions: jump,
            color: JUMP_EDGE_COLOR,
            no_depth_test: false,
        });
    }
}

fn segmentation_edges_overlay(mesh: &ConnectedMesh, ec: &EdgeClassification) -> viz::VizOverlay {
    let mut overlay = viz::VizOverlay::new();
    overlay.status_text = format!(
        "Stage 2.1: edge classification — {} sharp (red), {} curvature-jump (orange), {} interior [space=continue, q=quit]",
        ec.sharp_count, ec.jump_count, ec.interior_count,
    );
    push_boundary_lines(&mut overlay, mesh, ec);
    overlay
}

/// Deterministic distinct color for region `i`: golden-ratio hue stepping.
fn region_color(i: usize) -> [f32; 4] {
    let h = (i as f64 * 0.618_033_988_749_895).fract() * 6.0;
    let sector = (h.floor() as i32).rem_euclid(6);
    let f = h - h.floor();
    let (s, v) = (0.55, 0.9);
    let p = v * (1.0 - s);
    let q = v * (1.0 - s * f);
    let t = v * (1.0 - s * (1.0 - f));
    let (r, g, b) = match sector {
        0 => (v, t, p),
        1 => (q, v, p),
        2 => (p, v, t),
        3 => (p, q, v),
        4 => (t, p, v),
        _ => (v, p, q),
    };
    [r as f32, g as f32, b as f32, 1.0]
}

fn segmentation_regions_overlay(mesh: &ConnectedMesh, seg: &Segmentation) -> viz::VizOverlay {
    let mut overlay = viz::VizOverlay::new();
    let single = seg.regions.iter().filter(|r| r.faces.len() == 1).count();
    let largest = seg.regions.iter().map(|r| r.faces.len()).max().unwrap_or(0);
    overlay.status_text = format!(
        "Stage 2.2: {} regions ({} single-face, largest {} faces) — boundaries: sharp red / jump orange [space=continue, q=quit]",
        seg.regions.len(),
        single,
        largest,
    );
    overlay.face_highlights = seg
        .regions
        .iter()
        .enumerate()
        .map(|(i, r)| viz::FaceHighlight {
            face_indices: r.faces.clone(),
            color: region_color(i),
        })
        .collect();
    push_boundary_lines(&mut overlay, mesh, &seg.edge_classes);
    overlay
}

> The user is reporting that Kimi K3 tried multiple segmentation strategies and settled on an anchored osculating-jet estimator, but they're still seeing significant fragmentation issues on conical surfaces and planes near tangent surfaces. They're questioning whether to keep refining segmentation or switch to a seed-first multi-type competition approach instead, and they want my assessment. I need to review the development plan to give them a solid recommendation on which direction to pursue. merge repair can't fix is under-segmentation — if a true boundary is missing from the edge classification, no amount of merging recovers it. So the real question is whether the edge classifier is producing all the true boundaries, even if it's also producing false ones. If it is, then the fragmentation is just noise that the fitting loop cleans up; if it isn't, the architecture is fundamentally broken. The nosecone case suggests the classifier might be missing boundaries or creating spurious ones at a scale that makes the merge repair do most of the actual work rather than just polishing the result. If partition fragments the cone into 130 pieces, each small fragment fits a cylinder or plane within tolerance and gets classified as the simpler model — then merge has to somehow recognize that 130 cylinders/planes actually form a cone, which requires refitting at higher degrees of freedom. That's exactly the model-promotion machinery I'm trying to avoid, so the partition-first approach defeats its own purpose on these hard cases. essarily becomes fit-driven agglomerative merging with model promotion — which is the contingency algorithm, just seeded with segments instead of faces. When under-segmentation occurs, splitting also needs fit-validation-driven subdivision. So the architecture naturally converges toward fit-driven regardless of the initial approach; the real question is whether the fit-free partition serves as a useful preprocessing step that reduces complexity and leverages reliable sharp edges, rather than being the core mechanism itself. This suggests keeping the dependable parts — sharp-dihedral edges are exact and cheap — while treating the curvature-jump classifier as advisory rather than foundational.
> 
> A hybrid approach seems most promising: classify only sharp edges where the dihedral angle exceeds tolerance, which avoids curvature estimation entirely and stays robust. Then extract smooth regions as connected components across non-sharp edges. On sharp-edged models this directly gives the right answer, while on tangent-junction models the smooth regions become unions of tangentially-connected true regions like planes, fillets, and cylinder chains.
> 
> Within each smooth region, I'd run the fit-driven decomposition with seed-first multi-type competition restricted to that region, using curvature ordering only as a heuristic for seeding—where errors affect efficiency rather than correctness. Since sharp boundaries already isolate the smooth regions, competition only happens where it matters: at tangent junctions.
> 
> This hybrid approach scopes the contingency to smooth regions and gains real value: most models are decided by sharp edges alone, hypothesis competition never crosses sharp boundaries, and the per-seed competition cost stays localized. The curvature-jump classifier's role shifts to ordering seeds within smooth regions—its original successful function—where errors are harmless.
> 
> Now I need to look back at what the previous LLM concluded in the development plan and compare approaches. The anchor-without-rejection design trades away the ghost-boundary fix in favor of junction detection, but that same mechanism creates intermediate reads on junction rows that cause planes near junctions to see nonzero curvature and fragment internally. The previous analysis already flagged junction-circle confetti as bounded and the designed input for the repair loop, so I'm seeing a consistent pattern where every estimator approach introduces its own fragmentation signature. The boundary placement approach avoids curvature estimates entirely, which is its real advantage. The jet-based work validated that curvature works as a seeding heuristic and that region interiors are clean, but it can't serve as a decision rule at junctions — that's where the contingency's design shines by using curvature only for ordering, never for partitioning. This campaign confirms the contingency's core choices are sound, and I've hit the decision gate: if calibration doesn't produce reliable partitions, I switch to seed-first competition rather than continuing down the current path.

878	  triangle's plane is a chord whose normal is degrees off the true

902	### Segmentation-first vs. the Contingency — an honest verdict
903	
904	The Contingency (seed-first multi-type competition) places boundaries by
905	**fit validation at vertex tolerance** — exact, no curvature estimation
906	needed, so tangent junctions and junction circles come out clean by
907	construction. Segmentation-first places boundaries by **curvature
908	estimation**, which is fundamentally noisy at junction rows and undefined
909	on junction circles — producing ±1-row boundary error and seam confetti

912	On the other hand, segmentation-first's **fit support is maximal**:

914	strip problem (the Contingency still has it — from any elbow seed the
915	torus rung is fed a 1-ring and loses to a cylinder strip — unless the
916	degenerate-limit growth model is built too). And segmentation-first
917	keeps freeform first-class (a freeform region is a region; the
918	Contingency leaves freeform as leftover-gathering).
919	
920	**Verdict: they converge, and the deciding evidence doesn't exist yet.**
921	The Contingency wins on boundary exactness; segmentation-first wins on
922	fit conditioning and freeform. What segmentation-first has already

> The retrospective recommends treating builds 2.3 and 2.4 as the critical test—if the repair proves fragile or requires constant adjustment, we should fall back to the contingency plan rather than continuing to tweak the edge classifier. However, the user is reporting severe fragmentation across conical surfaces and planes near tangent surfaces, with the nosecone showing far more regions than expected.
> 
> Looking at the fragmentation counts across different parts—the stepped block, filleted pipe, and rounded cube all showing multiples of what we'd consider the true region count—I'm now examining whether the designed merge strategy can actually handle this. The merge is supposed to absorb smaller regions whose vertices validate against a larger, correctly-classified adjacent region's surface. This should work in two scenarios: junction confetti (small fragments next to large correct regions, where vertices lie on the seam or plane) and fragmented cone interiors, though the cone fragmentation is where things get tricky.
> 
> For the cone case, even if it shatters into dozens of moderately-sized fragments with no single dominant piece, each fragment still fits the same true cone with nearly identical parameters—so the merge test fires when a smaller fragment's vertices validate against a larger neighbor's fitted cone surface. The repair cascades through connected fragments as long as at least one is large enough to correctly identify as a cone, but it breaks down when all fragments are too small or narrow to classify as the true cone in the first place.
> 
> The real risk emerges with massive fragmentation into single-row bands that each look like cylinders—then no fragment carries the cone identity and merges deadlock at a cylinder tiling, which resurrects the old pipe-elbow problem. That's where model promotion kicks in, fitting the union at higher degrees of freedom. So the honest picture is that 2.4 handles the case where a dominant fragment exists, but with extreme fragmentation into bands, the risk is real. I should actually examine the data by running diagnostics on the nosecone and stepped block models to see the fragment size distribution on conical surfaces and determine whether the dominant-fragment condition holds.
> 
> On the user's second concern about planes adjacent to tangent surfaces fragmenting—planar fragments are actually the easiest repair case since every fragment fits the plane exactly and any fragment classifies as plane, so merges cascade trivially. That fragmentation is benign from the repair architecture's perspective, just visually annoying. The conical fragmentation is what needs investigation.
> 
> Let me check what test files are available for the nosecone cases, build the dump_regions tool, and run it in summary mode on the medium-sized test models to see what region counts we're getting.

//! Debug dump for stage 2.1/2.2 segmentation (scratch tool, not shipped).

use brepper::config;
use brepper::stage1::ConnectedMesh;
use brepper::stage2::edges::{classify_edges, EdgeClass};
use brepper::stage2::regions::extract_regions;
use brepper::stage1;

fn centroid(mesh: &ConnectedMesh, fi: usize) -> [f64; 3] {
    let f = &mesh.faces[fi];
    let vc = f.vertex_count as usize;
    let mut c = [0.0; 3];
    for &vi in &f.vertex_indices[..vc] {
        let v = &mesh.vertices[vi];
        c[0] += v.x;
        c[1] += v.y;
        c[2] += v.z;
    }
    let n = vc as f64;
    [c[0] / n, c[1] / n, c[2] / n]
}

fn main() {
    let args: Vec<String> = std::env::args().collect();
    let path = &args[1];
    let mode = args.get(2).map(|s| s.as_str()).unwrap_or("summary");
    let config = config::parse_config_from(["brepper", path, "-q"]).unwrap();
    let mesh = stage1::stage1(&config).unwrap();
    let ec = classify_edges(&mesh, config.angular_tolerance_rad);

        "{} faces, {} regions: sharp={} jump={} interior={} ({} rounds)",

        ec.sharp_count,
        ec.jump_count,
        ec.interior_count,
        ec.rounds
    );

            let mut order: Vec<usize> = (0..regions.len()).collect();

**My verdict: pivot — but a scoped pivot, not a wholesale abandonment.** The campaign's own data, which I verified directly with `dump_regions`, shows the curvature-jump classifier is unsalvageable as the *load-bearing* boundary mechanism. But roughly 80% of what was built survives the pivot, and the previous LLM's retrospective is honest and mostly right — I part ways with it only on "build 2.3/2.4 next as the make-or-break test."

## The decisive evidence

I ran the diagnostics myself on the models you flagged, and the fragmentation pattern is more damning than the retrospective's summary suggests:

**The classifier fires on real curvature gradients — a false-positive class, not a tuning miss.** On nosecone_medium (980 faces, 130 regions), the sphere comes out as one clean 644-face region reading σ=(0.080, 0.080) — exactly 1/12.5, the estimator working beautifully. The entire fragmentation tail is the cone, and the fragments' descriptors tell the story: (0.00, 0.00) at the wide base → (0.02, 0.00) mid-cone → (0.08, 0.00) near the tip. That's κ_max = 1/ρ(h) — the *correct* reading of a cone, whose principal curvature genuinely varies continuously along the axis. The estimator is measuring the truth; the *decision rule* (threshold on first differences of curvature) cannot distinguish "continuous gradient on one surface" from "G2 jump between two surfaces." Your fragmented planes are the same statistic failing in its other mode near junction contamination.

**The two error classes overlap in the decision statistic — no threshold exists.** The retrospective records pipe_elbow's genuine torus↔cylinder junction reading a jump ratio of 0.33, below FIRE=0.35 (missed → walls merge into the bend). Meanwhile the cone's internal gradient steps read *above* 0.35 (fired → shatter). Any threshold below 0.33 shreds cones worse; any threshold above 0.35 misses real junctions. This is not a calibration problem — the statistic itself can't separate the classes, and a sixth estimator iteration (second differences, gradient-aware tests) would be fighting the same noise floor on staggered tessellations that killed the first five.

**Supporting signals:** the hysteresis fixpoint hits its 10-round cap (i.e., oscillates without converging) on every fragmenting model — nosecone, cone_truncated, filleted_pipe — and converges only where jump=0. And five estimator variants each fixed one corpus class while breaking another, which is the signature of a signal that genuinely isn't in the local data at junction-row scale. Contrast: cone_15x20 and simple_cone segment *perfectly* with jump playing no role at all — sharp edges alone, one round, zero new knobs.

## Where I differ from the retrospective

It says "build 2.3/2.4 next — that's the make-or-break test." I'd call the test already decided, for two reasons. First, the plan's own roadmap step 1 set the gate explicitly: *"if no calibration produces reliable partitions on the corpus, switch to seed-first multi-type competition rather than building 2.3/2.4 on bad partitions."* That gate has triggered by its own criterion. Second, look at what 2.4 repair would actually be doing: the fit-validated **merge** probably *can* reassemble the cone confetti (each fragment refits the true cone by exactness, and merges cascade) — but **split** inherits the broken statistic. For pipe_elbow's under-segmentation, "re-run the classifier at lowered thresholds" would shred the bend's internal gradients along with the junction, and merge would then rebuild from confetti. In that architecture, 100% of the information doing real work is fit validation at vertex tolerance; the curvature classifier is just generating noise for fitting to clean up. When that's the honest description, the honest architecture is fit-driven decomposition.

## The scoped pivot

Keep what the campaign proved, discard only the broken piece:

- **Keep**: sharp-edge classification + region extraction (exact on the entire sharp-edged corpus — most models — with no knobs beyond the user-facing angular tolerance), the `Segmentation` infrastructure, viz, `dump_regions`, the 14 tests, the invariants. The mesh partitions into **smooth regions** bounded by sharp edges.
- **Demote the jet to a prior**: it's a genuinely good estimator — region interiors read clean — and "interior-first seed ordering" is exactly the role where its errors cost efficiency, never correctness. That was the contingency's stated graceful-degradation property all along.
- **Pivot junction discovery to fit validation**: within each smooth region, first try the classification ladder on the *whole region* (this bags spheres, isolated cylinders, most of the corpus in one shot, preserving the maximal-fit-support win the retrospective correctly celebrates); when the whole region fits nothing, run the contingency's seed-first multi-type competition *inside that region* — jet-ordered seeds, all types compete, largest-validated-area wins, boundaries fall out of exact vertex distances. Scoping the competition inside smooth regions fixes the contingency's two cost concerns: no cross-sharp-edge accidents, bounded competition arena.
- **Honest new cost**: the degenerate-limit growth model (k=1/R torus parameterization, chordal refit triggers, ridge regularization) moves from "contingency upgrade" to critical path — the elbow bend inside its smooth region is still a strip mesh, and seed-fed fits still need to discover the bend. That's the one significant piece of new machinery the pivot commits us to.
- **Unchanged**: fit-validated merge + boundary relabel + the three invariants + the 2.5 output contract — these were common to both architectures from the start, so nothing there is wasted.

The retrospective's closing insight — "they converge, and the deciding evidence doesn't exist yet" — was right about the convergence and wrong only about the evidence: it exists now, and the convergence point is fit validation with curvature as a prior.

If you're convinced, I'd update DEVELOPMENT_PLAN (promote the contingency to primary with the smooth-region scoping, move the curvature-jump classifier to Abandoned Approaches with this evidence recorded) and start on the scoped competition. Want me to proceed with that?

I'm thinking through what these signatures actually enable: they can classify surface types based on classical curvature-space patterns—planes show (0, 0), cylinders show one zero and one constant, cones show one zero with spatial variation, spheres show equal constants, and tori show one constant with one varying component including sign changes. This classification could serve as a prior for surface-type prediction and seed ordering.
> 
> Instead of running all 5-6 surface fits per seed region (the current bottleneck), the jet pre-classifier can rank which types are plausible based on the local curvature signature and skip clearly inconsistent ones. That cuts the per-seed competition cost from roughly 5 expansions down to 2, giving a direct speedup while remaining safe because we're just reordering, not eliminating valid candidates.
> 
> Beyond type filtering, the jet data also provides initial parameter estimates that seed the optimization — radius estimates from principal curvatures, sphere center positions from the normal direction, and for torus fitting, the tube radius and medial axis information that the existing fitter already uses.
> 
> The principal directions from the jet's Hessian eigenvectors directly give axis orientations: the zero-curvature direction aligns with cylinder/cone rulings, while the maximum-curvature direction wraps around a torus tube. Using these across multiple faces creates a more stable axis initializer than relying on normal covariance alone, especially on narrow geometry.
> 
> During BFS expansion, I can use this geometric information to gate which candidate faces to accept based on how well they align with the predicted type and parameters. A jet-based pre-filter could skip expensive refit attempts on faces that don't match the current growth model—for instance, rejecting a planar face (0,0 curvature) adjacent to a cylinder rather than wasting computation on promotion refits. The catch is that junction rows have contaminated jet readings, so a hard gate at boundaries would incorrectly filter candidates that might actually belong.
> 
> The solution is to keep the gate loose (factor-of-2 to 3 bands rather than tight thresholds) and let the vertex validation be the final arbiter—the jet gate only gates refit *attempts*, never outright rejects a face that passes vertex checks. Skipped refits at junctions are harmless anyway since the shared-claim machinery resolves those faces downstream.
> 
> For stopping conditions, the jet signature could signal when growth should halt (like when a sphere hits faces with different curvature), but the vertex test already handles this precisely, so the jet mainly saves computation by stopping early.
> 
> The real insight is moving from a generic classifier to type-conditioned thresholds: once you hypothesize a surface type (say, a cylinder with radius r), you know exactly what the jet should measure on member faces—specific curvature and torsion values within tolerance—rather than just comparing differences between neighbors. This model-conditioned expectation is far more discriminating than generic neighbor-to-neighbor comparisons.
> 
> For a cone, the model predicts the exact curvature gradient as a function of height, so the false-positive class of gradient-like faces vanishes because the expectation tracks the actual geometry. The classifier failed before because it was model-free; conditioning on a hypothesis makes curvature a well-posed test.
> 
> This mirrors the old pipeline's normal-axis consistency check—that was also model-conditioned and worked well. So model-conditioned bands around predicted principal curvatures are robust and work per surface type, but fit-free absolute thresholds per type fail because radii vary wildly and junctions contaminate the data.
> 
> Now looking at the computational cost: the jet computation is already O(N·ring) per iteration, taking about 0.2 seconds for 10.8k faces, and precomputing once would eliminate that overhead.
> 
> The speed wins come from several places—rung pruning cuts expansions by 2-3×, refit gating avoids expensive Levenberg-Marquardt refits which is probably the biggest practical gain, better initialization from jet principal directions and medial points reduces LM iterations and rescue ladder calls, and seed ordering is already established. But the jet can't actually decide boundaries or membership—that still requires vertex-tolerance validation.
> 
> Jet data tells the algorithm *what to try* (types, parameters, order, whether to refit), while fitting determines *what's true*. For the degenerate-limit growth model, jet gives per-face medial points that let me estimate spine curvature directly by fitting a circle or line to those points—more robust than the old pairwise normal-line intersection method, which fails on strips where adjacent normals are nearly parallel.
> 
> On tube strips, the jet sidesteps that weakness entirely since it doesn't need non-parallel normal pairs. For the elbow geometry, each strip face reads the tube radius κ₁ directly, the medial points become tube centers, and fitting the spine to accumulated medial points as the support grows naturally implements the k=1/R continuation. The jet essentially hands the growth model its state estimate. I'm also checking the jet's accuracy on strips against the retrospective data—the pipe_elbow case was designed exactly for this scenario, and the torus readings (1/r_tube with correct sign changes) look correct.
> 
> On the corpus's hardest tessellations, the jet still delivers usable values in the interior regions, though junction rows are contaminated by design since they're anchored at mixing boundaries. There's a caveat though: at the cone tip, the base-end reads nearly zero because the radius ρ is around 60mm, giving κ≈0.017, which rounds to zero at the histogram resolution of 0.02. The descriptor bins at 1/50, so it's reading roughly 0.01-ish—very large radius plus large facets means the jet might genuinely read near zero versus the true 0.017.
> 
> For rung-pruning, if a face would predict "plane," I try the plane fit first, but it fails at the vertex tolerance since the sagitta exceeds 5e-5 over 20mm facets, so I fall through the ladder and preserve correctness with minimal cost—a good example of graceful degradation. The threshold bands need to account for the jet's known error sources: junction rows up to intermediate levels, slivers that the design handles, and coarse tessellation with few samples, so bands like [0.3×, 3×] are expected, or better yet, use them only for skip-refit decisions and exploration ordering.
> 
> On the user's specific request about guiding seed growth, I covered the candidate ordering by expanding into curvature-consistent faces first so the region core assembles before ambiguous junction rows, which improves refit stability since refits start from a more stable foundation.
> 
> The growth can also use the jet to decide when to attempt model promotion: if a cylinder stalls and frontier faces read a small nonzero curvature, that signals the tube continues but bends, so I promote to a bent-tube or torus and refit; if frontier reads zero curvature, it's a plane junction and I stop without promoting. This gives a clean trigger heuristic for degenerate-limit models—the jet tells the grower which limit to escape toward, and the fit validates it. Promotion triggers were an open design point before (refits triggered by chordal proximity or batched per stalled frontier were expensive), but jet-informed triggers are much cheaper.
> 
> So the precomputed jet data has four solid roles: seed ordering, rung pruning and initialization, refit and promotion gating at stalled frontiers, and model-conditioned membership pre-filtering. Per-type thresholds work when conditioned on a fitted model with generous bands around predicted curvatures, but not as fit-free absolutes. The key invariant is that the jet never decides membership or boundaries—vertex-tolerance validation does that; the jet only allocates effort. This keeps the failure mode that killed the classifier out of the loop, because jet errors convert to wasted or saved time instead of wrong geometry.
> 
> Speed-wise, rung pruning gives roughly a 2× improvement, refit gating is the big win for growth-model-heavy cases, and initialization eliminates rescue ladders. This also recovers and formalizes several old heuristics: normal-axis consistency becomes model-conditioned curvature bands, medial-axis radius estimation maps to jet κ1, and curvature seed ordering stays unchanged. ## 🤖 Assistant
<!-- msg_011CdERxR2gKuBhW7Xfq7EAR -->

Yes to all three — and this is exactly the right question, because it defines the jet's proper place in the pivoted architecture. The one-sentence version: **the jet should allocate effort, never decide geometry.** Every role below converts jet error into wasted-or-saved time; the vertex-tolerance fit validation stays the sole decider of membership and boundaries. That division keeps the failure mode that killed the classifier permanently out of the loop.

## What precomputed jet data buys, concretely

**1. Rung pruning and ordering (per-seed speedup, ~2–3×).** The (κ₁, κ₂) signature predicts surface type directly: (0,0) plane; (κ,0) with κ constant across the neighborhood → cylinder; (κ,0) with κ drifting → cone; κ₁≈κ₂ → sphere; both nonzero and unequal → torus/freeform. Instead of the contingency's ~5 fit-and-expands per seed, run the 1–2 signature-consistent types first and fall through to the full ladder only on validation failure. A misprediction (e.g., the nosecone's wide-end cone faces reading ≈(0,0) → try plane first → plane fails at vertex tolerance instantly) costs one cheap failed fit, never a wrong region.

**2. Fit initialization (kills the rescue ladders).** The jet hands the fitters their starting state: r ≈ 1/κ₁ for cylinders/spheres; sphere center ≈ p − n/κ₁; and — the underused part — the jet Hessian's *eigenvectors* give principal directions, so the zero-curvature direction is the ruling/generator of a cylinder or cone, and per-face medial points p + n/κ₁ are tube centers for the torus. That last one directly repairs a known weakness: the existing medial-axis torus fitter estimates the minor radius from pairwise normal-line intersections and must reject near-parallel normal pairs (|n₁·n₂| > 0.95) — which is exactly what strip meshes are full of. Jet κ₁ gives the same quantity per face with no pairing at all. Better initialization is why the multi-axis torus rescue and much of the LM patience machinery existed; a lot of that should become unnecessary.

**3. Growth guidance — refit gating and promotion triggers (the big one).** The expensive operation in fit-driven growth isn't the per-face vertex check (four point evaluations); it's the LM *refit* attempted when a frontier face near-misses. The degenerate-limit growth model made this worse — its refit trigger had to loosen to chordal tolerance, inviting many attempts. The jet is the cheap discriminator at a stalled frontier:

- Cylinder growth stalls; frontier faces read (1/r, small-but-nonzero) → "the tube continues but bends" → attempt the k=1/R promotion refit. And the accumulated medial points of the grown support give the spine-curvature estimate to seed it — the jet essentially hands the bent-tube model its state.
- Frontier reads (0,0) → it's the adjacent plane → stop; don't spend a promotion refit.
- Within-region candidate *ordering*: expand into curvature-consistent faces first, so refits always run from clean core support and ambiguous junction rows arrive last (when the model is already well-determined and the exact vertex test resolves them trivially).

This answers the open design question in the growth model ("what triggers promotion?") with something targeted instead of blanket chordal-proximity refitting.

**4. Seed ordering** — already established; interiors-first via the jet is its original, validated role.

## "Are there thresholds which work for a given surface type?"

As **model-conditioned bands, yes; as fit-free absolutes, no** — and the distinction is precisely why this works where the classifier failed. The classifier had to answer "is this neighbor-difference a junction?" with no model, and the two answer classes overlap in that statistic (the 0.33-vs-0.35 kill shot). But once a hypothesis exists, the *model predicts what the jet should read on every member face* — including gradients: a fitted cone predicts (cos α/ρ(h), 0) with ρ varying along the axis, so the exact false-positive class that shattered the nosecone disappears, because the expectation tracks the gradient. Against a model, generous bands work fine — jet interiors are accurate to a few % and junction rows are off by up to ~half, so something like [⅓×, 3×] around the predicted values cleanly separates "plausibly this surface, worth the vertex check / refit" from "obviously not, skip." Note this is the old pipeline's one genuinely effective consistency heuristic (|n·axis| = sin α, the normal-axis check) reborn in curvature space with proper theoretical footing.

The corpus's measured jet quality supports this: interiors read clean on every hard tessellation the campaign fought (65:1 slivers, staggered rows, full-length strips), and the contaminated reads are confined to junction rows — which under this design never face a jet decision anyway, because membership there is settled by the exact test and the relabel machinery.

## Net effect

Precompute once (one pass, sharp-edge exclusion only — no fixpoint needed since nothing iterates on classification anymore; ~0.2s at 10k faces), attach (κ₁, κ₂) + principal directions + medial point per face to the `Segmentation`, and thread it through as: seed order → rung filter → fit initializer → refit/promotion gate. Expected wins: per-seed competition cost roughly halved or better, LM rescue ladders mostly retired, promotion refits fired only where curvature says bending is plausible — and zero correctness exposure, because every jet-informed decision either gets validated by fitting or was merely an ordering choice.

So the jet work wasn't a dead end at all — it built the *prior* the fit-driven architecture wants. It just can't be the judge. If you want, I'll fold this into the plan's pivoted-architecture section (jet roles, model-conditioned bands, promotion triggers) as part of writing up the pivot.

257	  facet normal** — the smallest-variance direction of the neighborhood IS
258	  the local normal and stays accurate on strips. Own vertices dominate the
259	  frame (weight \`OWN_WEIGHT\`).
260	- **The face's own vertices anchor the fit** (weight \`OWN_WEIGHT = 50\`).
261	  Tessellators never straddle a B-rep boundary with a triangle, so a face's
262	  own corners always lie on its own surface — this biases each face's fit
263	  toward its own side at a junction, which is what makes junction rows read
264	  *different* intermediate values and the boundary fire. There is **no
265	  outlier rejection**: rejection forces arbitrary one-side reads that vary
266	  face to face and fragment tangent junctions (measured); sharp-crease
267	  neighbors (dihedral > \`--angular-tolerance\`) are excluded from the point
268	  set instead, and tangent-junction mixing is allowed (intermediate reads).
269	
270	The estimator runs inside a fixpoint iteration with the classification
271	(below): round 1 admits all non-sharp neighbors; once junctions fire,
272	cross-boundary faces leave the neighborhood and the descriptors purify.
273	
274	*History of the estimator, for context:* per-edge κ = θ/d failed on
275	irregular tessellation (the centroid distance doesn't measure arc length in
276	the rotation direction — the 65:1 sliver fillet of rounded_cube_10_r2_fine
277	fragmented ~50×). The neighbor-normal tensor fixed strips and slivers but
278	deadlocked on balanced-mix junction rings (compromise neighbors agreeing
279	with each other, e.g. stepped_block's blend zones). The jet subsumes both.
280	
281	**Classification rules (per edge):**
282	1. **Sharp**: \`|θ_e| > --angular-tolerance\` → BOUNDARY (θ_e = dihedral).
283	   Catches every non-tangent junction. The default 25° stays above the
284	   tessellator's maximum dihedral step on smooth surfaces (coarse fillets
285	   step ~24°/row).
286	2. **Curvature jump** (tangent junctions, G1-smooth but G2-discontinuous):
287	   with \`jump(e) = max(|σ_max(f1) − σ_max(f2)|, |σ_min(f1) − σ_min(f2)|)\`
288	   and \`scale(e) = max(|σ_max|, |σ_min| over both faces)\` — the
289	   local-relative criterion, since mesh-global thresholds are dominated by
290	   sharp creases. The rule iterates to a fixpoint with **hysteresis**:
291	   FIRE when \`jump > 0.35 · scale\` and \`jump > κ_floor\`; UN-FIRE when
292	   \`jump < 0.15 · scale\` or \`jump < κ_floor\`; otherwise keep the previous
293	   state (cap 10 rounds; convergence typically 2–4).
294	3. Everything else → INTERIOR.
295	
296	\`κ_floor = C · ε / d_e²\` with ε = f32 position quantization (bbox diagonal ×
297	1e-7 — STL vertices are f32-exact; NOT \`--vertex-tolerance\`, whose 5e-5
298	over-floors sliver-dense areas and vetoed real junctions at triple points),
299	\`C\` calibrated on the corpus.
300	
301	**Why the two-sided descriptor matters:** \`σ_max\` alone misses tangent
302	junctions between surfaces sharing their largest curvature — the capsule
303	(pill) model's hemisphere↔cylinder junction has \`σ_max = 1/r\` on both sides,
304	but \`σ_min\` jumps from \`1/r\` (sphere) to \`0\` (cylinder), which rule 2
305	catches at full strength (verified: pill_fine produces exactly 3 regions;
306	pill_coarse 3 true regions + junction-circle singletons). Fillet↔plane
307	(\`σ_max\`: \`1/r\` vs 0) and fillet↔cylinder with sufficiently different radii
308	fire on \`σ_max\`. A fillet↔cylinder pair with similar radii (e.g.
309	\`1 − r/R < J\`) can slip
310	through — that miss is recovered by stage 2.4's split repair, which re-runs
311	this classifier at progressively lower \`J\` inside regions that fail to
312	classify.
313	
314	**Expected over-fire:** on coarse cones near the apex, \`σ_max\` varies
315	row-to-row (circumferential curvature grows toward the apex), so rule 2 may
316	split one cone into bands. Harmless: each band classifies as the same cone
317	and stage 2.4's merge rejoins them on parameter agreement. Junction-zone
318	slivers at tangent junctions and triple points (faces whose neighbor rings
319	conflict) likewise produce small confetti regions — the merge repair's
320	designed input.
321	
322	Output: \`EdgeClassification\` keyed by undirected mesh edge, retaining the
323	class and the curvature evidence (\`κ_e\`, per-face descriptors) for reuse by
324	the 2.4 split repair, \`--compare\` diagnostics, and viz. Deterministic, O(E).
325	
326	#### 2.2 Region extraction
327	
328	Connected components of the face graph over INTERIOR edges (union-find).
329	
330	Output (in \`Stage2Output\`):
331	- \`face_region: Vec<usize>\` — exactly one region per face (total coverage by
332	  construction).
333	- \`regions: Vec<Region>\` — member faces, member vertices, and ordered
334	  boundary loops (mesh-vertex loops per region, shared with the freeform
335	  parameterizer and structurally the same boundaries stage 3.1 will chain).
336	
337	Regions are connected by construction. There is no minimum size: a single
338	facet is a valid region (and classifies as its own plane — this is the
339	terminal fallback that guarantees every model converts).
340	
341	**Diagnostics** (report-only at this stage; repair may still move
342	boundaries): with \`--compare\`, sample midpoints of BOUNDARY edges and check
343	distance to the nearest reference STEP *edge* (within \`--surface-tolerance\` —
344	tangent-junction boundaries can legitimately sit one facet row off). Report
345	counts of suspicious boundary edges and of regions whose faces project onto
346	reference faces with disagreeing surface geometry (a missed-seam signal).
347	
348	#### 2.3 Region classification
349	
350	For each region, fit surface types in **ascending degrees of freedom** and
351	accept the first fit that validates. Simplest-first resolves degeneracy (any
352	plane also lies on an infinite-radius cylinder; a single facet is exactly a
353	plane) without coverage heuristics.
354	
355	**The ladder:**
356	
357	| Rung | Surface | DOF | Fit (see SURFACE_FITTING.md) |
358	|------|---------|-----|------------------------------|
359	| 1 | Plane | 3 | Area-weighted normal average |
360	| 2 | Sphere | 4 | Algebraic LSQ + Gauss-Newton |
361	| 3 | Cylinder | 5 | Normal-covariance axis + 2D circle fit + LM |
362	| 4 | Cone | 6 | Two-path (pointed/truncated) + LM; the existing code fits cylinder and cone together and this rung keeps that dual-fit internally, preferring the cylinder when both validate |
363	| 5 | Torus | 7 | Medial-axis tube-center + SOR axis + multi-axis LM rescue |
364	| 6 | Freeform B-spline | — | See "Freeform (B-spline) fitting" below; entered only via the 2.4 split-probe protocol |
365	
366	**Validation (per fit, decisive by Design Principle #1):**
367	- Every region vertex within \`--vertex-tolerance\` of the surface.
368	- Every face centroid within \`--surface-tolerance\` (chordal sagitta ≈ h²/8R
369	  stays well inside for real tessellations).
370	- Orientation coherence: every face normal agrees in sign with the surface
371	  normal at its centroid — this determines the hypothesis \`convex\` flag and
372	  replaces the old per-face BFS convexity checks (it is what keeps the inner
373	  and outer walls of a thin pipe from ever sharing a surface; they are
374	  separate regions anyway, since the wall rim makes sharp edges).
375	- Numeric-validity guards retained from the fitters (these are fit-domain
376	  constraints, not selection heuristics): cone half-angle in [2°, 85°],
377	  torus \`0 < r ≤ R\`, sphere radius ≤ 1000× bounding-box diagonal.
378	
379	**Fits see the whole region.** Initial parameter estimates use all faces
380	(area-weighted normal covariance, etc.), which conditions far better than
381	the abandoned pipeline's 3-face seeds — e.g. the pipe-elbow torus, which
382	previously needed special strip-merge seeding because narrow cylinder strips
383	had already tiled it, is now fitted once from the full tube and the (h,r)
384	profile is unambiguous. For large regions, fit on a deterministic vertex
385	subsample, validate against all vertices, and refit augmented with the worst
386	offenders on a near-miss (bounded retries).
387	
388	**Sub-tolerance features**: when the fitted curvature radius is below
389	\`--surface-tolerance\` (3DBenchy has fitted cylinders of r=0.2mm against the
390	0.4mm default), centroid checks are vacuous at that scale; classify by
391	vertex tolerance alone and mark the region, since stage 3's chain validation
392	has the same blind spot there (see TODO, face-5841 family).
393	
394	**Output**: exactly one hypothesis per region, appended to the typed vectors
395	in \`Stage2Output\` (\`planar_hypotheses\`, \`conical_hypotheses\` — cylinders are
396	cones with \`half_angle == 0\` — \`spherical_hypotheses\`,
397	\`toroidal_hypotheses\`, \`bspline_hypotheses\`). Hypothesis structs keep their
398	existing fields: type-specific parameters, \`faces\`, \`vertices\`, \`convex\`,
399	and error statistics (\`error_max\`, \`centroid_error_max\`).
400	
401	##### Freeform (B-spline) fitting
402	
403	The final rung, reached only after the 2.4 split-probe protocol has ruled
404	out "this region is really several analytic surfaces with a missed seam".
405	The fitting machinery carries over intact from the previous architecture
406	(implemented 2026-07-10 through 2026-07-15, \`src/stage2/bspline.rs\`); what
407	is deleted is its old region-*gathering* front end (see Abandoned
408	Approaches) — the partition now supplies the region. A planar margin of a
409	freeform surface is curvature-continuous with it and lands in the same
410	region automatically, which is what the old "tangent-plane merge" step
411	existed to fix.
412	
413	**Algorithm (\`deduce_bspline_surfaces\`, per region):**
414	1. Regions planar to fitting exactness (10× vertex tolerance, NaN-guarded:
415	   the area-weighted normal of a closed region cancels to zero) become a
416	   multi-face planar hypothesis instead — a plane is preferable to a
417	   B-spline that overfits noise. Regions below 4 faces are not fitted (the
418	   2.4 fallback ladder handles them).
419	2. **Parameterize** the region onto the unit square: outer boundary loop pinned by cumulative chord length, 4 corners chosen where the adjacent *outside* region changes (arc-length-quartile fallback when the boundary offers ≠ 4 transitions), interior and hole-loop vertices solved with Floater mean-value weights by Gauss–Seidel (convergence enforced — an unconverged map voids Floater's bijectivity guarantee). Quads are split along the v0–v2 diagonal for weighting and sampling. **Closed bands** (a second boundary loop of extent ≥ half the largest — a small hole floats freely, a comparable second loop means the region wraps, e.g. the wall of a closed prism) are first **seam-cut** into a disk along a shortest vertex path between the two loops (\`cut_region_seam\`): the path follows FACE perimeter edges only (a path along a quad's split diagonal would tear that quad across the cut), its vertices are duplicated, right-side triangle fans switch to the clones, and the boundary re-chains into one loop whose two cut copies carry a \`CUT_OUTSIDE\` id — corner detection then lands the 4 corners exactly at the cut endpoints. The clones keep their originals' positions, so the two parameter-extreme grid columns of the around-the-band axis carry identical data (the periodic fit drops the closing one; the clamped fallback fit keeps both and closes exactly through the shared column).
420	3. **Resample a grid** through the flattened triangulation (uniform-bin point location; numeric misses near corner slivers are clamped to the nearest triangle within one bin — feeding them to the hole membrane extrapolates garbage). Grid stations per axis are the boundary vertices' own pinned positions (stations *between* the vertices of a coarse profile sample chord interiors and pin the fit to the chordal prism — a 1.7% volume deficit on the arch, the mesh's own chordal error), subdivided up to an interior-density target of ~1.5·√(region vertices) (a flat margin tessellates coarsely: hill_with_hole has 8-vertex sides on a 946-vertex region). Station spacing is **conditioned in 3D-arc measure** (\`axis_stations\`): chord-length-parametrized C2 interpolation amplifies data noise by roughly the square of the adjacent knot-spacing ratio, so a boundary mixing fine facets with one long straight chord (the xyz-cube letter walls: a 21× jump) turned a 0.02 mm-flat ribbon into control points 150 mm off the part while passing through every grid node exactly. Near-coincident stations (< 0.25× the median arc gap — the near-singular end of the same conditioning problem) are merged, and segments are subdivided to a fixpoint where no piece exceeds 2× either neighbor segment's pieces, uniformly in arc rather than UV (the Floater map compresses high-curvature areas). Surviving boundary-vertex stations keep their exact pinned positions; new substations land only inside long segments, where a long boundary chord means the mesh itself is flat and mid-segment samples lie on the true surface. Genuine hole interiors are filled with a Laplacian membrane so the fitter sees smooth data. The grid is flipped if needed so d1u×d1v matches the mesh's outward normals: B-spline faces are always \`concave = false\`.
421	4. **Fit**: \`GeomAPI_PointsToBSplineSurface::Interpolate\` (chord-length, C2 cubic) first — the grid is sampled from an exact-on-surface mesh, and OCCT's least-squares approximator reports \`IsDone\` while overshooting its \`Tol3D\` by millimeters near strongly curved patch boundaries. The approximator (tol = surface/8, then surface/2) remains as fallback, and grid density retries at caps 121/181/241. **Closed bands fit periodically**: \`Interpolate(…, thePeriodic=true)\` with the around-the-band axis as the array's FIRST index (transposed when that axis is v; the outward-orientation flip then reverses the second index and accounts for the transpose's normal negation) and the closing station omitted — OCCT appends the wrap-around copy of the first row itself and calls \`SetUPeriodic()\`. The periodic path is interpolation-only (the approximator binding carries no periodic flag). The resulting closure is C1, not C2 (position and D1 match across the wrap to ~1e-14; D2 jumps — measured), which nothing downstream requires. The fitted around-axis knots are then rescaled to exactly [0, 2π] via \`SetUKnots\` — an exact affine reparameterization (~1e-15 drift, measured) — because stage 3.4's wrapped-face machinery hard-codes that period. The U=0 iso is then exactly the cut path (chord-length parameters start at the first supplied row), a free seam gauge.
422	5. **Validate**: first a **control-point bound check** — every pole must lie within the grid data's bounding box inflated by half its diagonal. An ill-conditioned fit passes through every grid node exactly yet swings wildly between them, and the vertex/centroid validation below samples at data points, making it structurally blind to inter-node excursions; the surface lies in the convex hull of its poles, so poles near the data bound the whole surface near the data (the xyz-cube blow-up put poles 22 diagonals out; legit fits, including periodic bands, stay well inside the margin). Then every region vertex and face centroid within \`--surface-tolerance\` of the fit, each projection seeded from the vertex's own parameterization value (sequential seeding breaks at mesh row wraps, and neither \`NextValueOfUV\` nor \`ValueOfUV\` recovers reliably on many-knot surfaces). On failure at every grid density, the region returns to the 2.4 fallback ladder.
423	6. **Closed-band result**: the periodic fit produces ONE \`BSplineHypothesis\` with \`u_periodic = true\`, reconstructed by stage 3 as one wrapped face with a real seam edge — the form CAD kernels (and the references) write. If the periodic fit fails validation at every grid density, the band retries with the clamped fit and is **seam-split** (\`split_cut_hypothesis\`) into two \`SelectedSurface::BSpline\` halves sharing the fitted surface object; their two cut edges are plain edges that a reference model does not have, so the stage 3.1 edge compare will (correctly) flag them — the fallback trades compare-exactness for robustness and is currently exercised by no model.
424	
425	The \`BSplineHypothesis\` owns the fitted \`Geom_BSplineSurface\` (stage 3 reuses the same object via fresh handles) plus a cached, seed-rechecking \`ShapeAnalysis_Surface\` projector used by the 2.4 repair operations, \`--compare\`, and stage-3 charts/normals/projections. Stage-3 charts of B-spline faces also carry a parameter-space \`home\` anchor (the projection of the region's mean face centroid): a projection landing on a coincident-boundary curve of a seam-split band (the fallback) is ambiguous between the two parameter extremes (Newton, the gap re-check, and \`ValueOfUV\` all cannot tell — both branches are exact), and the branch nearer the face's own interior wins. On a periodic (u_periodic) chart the U-arm of that resolution is replaced by principal-value reduction to [0, 2π) — the same contract as the analytic periodic charts — and the whole wrapped-face machinery (edge-sample branch unwrap, winding classification, seam planning/anchoring, pcurve shifts) applies verbatim. There is no compare exemption: the stage 3.1 edge/vertex compare is fully armed for every edge, including the seam (a real feature both the reconstruction and the reference have).
426	
427	**Periodic-band junctions (stage 3.3)**: the band's two cap junctions are closed-loop edges (no vertices) and must carry PERIODIC curves — seam planning re-anchors a closed edge with \`MakeEdge\` over [t, t+period] at a solved seam azimuth, which a closed-but-non-periodic curve refuses. Two mechanisms deliver that (both were needed on elliptical_cylinder): (a) an IntSS curve that comes back closed-but-non-periodic (observed type: \`Geom_TrimmedCurve\`, exact to ~1e-14) is converted via \`GeomConvert::CurveToBSplineCurve\` + \`SetPeriodic\` (exact, measured); (b) an IntSS curve that comes back as a genuine partial arc falls to the boundary-chain repair, whose closed-chain arm interpolates a periodic C2 B-spline through the chain vertices (\`GeomAPI_Interpolate\` with the periodic flag, closing point not repeated — ~4e-15 chain deviation observed). Partial-arc detection for closed loops keys on the ENDPOINT GAP, not the parameter span: a closed B-spline junction arrives over an arbitrary range (observed [0,1]), and the old span≠2π test replaced the true junction with a bogus circle (14.8 mm off, 12% volume error).
428	
429	**Periodic closed-band conventions (probed 2026-07-15, \`tmp/probe_periodic.rs\`; confirmed in \`GeomAPI_PointsToBSplineSurface.cxx\`)**: periodic along U = the array's FIRST index; supply each row once (OCCT appends the wrap-around copy of row 1 itself; a duplicated closing row would make the system singular); the result already has \`SetUPeriodic()\` applied. The around-the-band axis is NOT always U in the Floater square (elliptical_cylinder's cut copies land as the u-extreme sides, making v the around axis): \`cut_around_axis\` detects it from the \`CUT_OUTSIDE\` loop entries, and \`fit_grid\` transposes the array so the surface's U is always the around axis — \`BSplineHypothesis.u_periodic\` is a single flag, no per-axis plumbing. The stage-3.4 wrapped-face machinery (branch unwrap in \`sample_edge_uv\`, winding classification, \`align_loop\` offsets, pcurve shifts, seam handling — \`grep TWO_PI src/stage3.rs\`) hard-codes the 2π period; \`SetUKnots\` rescales the fitted knots exactly, after which it all applies verbatim once the chart reports \`u_periodic()\`. Debug probes: \`BREPPER_DEBUG_EDGE_UV=1\` prints every chart projection during edge sampling; \`--stage=3.4 -v\` prints per-edge pcurve deviations + UV endpoints per face; the \`-vvv\` fit-residual-vs-own-grid probe separates fit error from sampler/projection error.
430	
431	**Implementation notes (from the stage-3 rework, 2026-07-08; all implemented — kept for reference):**
432	- *The chart contract.* A chart provides \`point_uv(p) → (u, v, pole_radius)\` and \`point_at(u, v)\`, plus \`u_periodic()\`/\`v_periodic()\`. For a B-spline chart: \`pole_radius\` is ∞ (like the plane chart — fitted height-field patches have no UV degeneracies), and **both periodic flags must be false for open patches** (\`u_periodic()\` is true only for the periodic closed-band fit). Every mod-2π unwrap/branch-lift in stage 3.4 is gated on those flags; reducing non-periodic parameter coordinates mod 2π silently corrupts them (a real bug on plane charts once, masked for months — see LEARNINGS). With both flags false, the whole seam/winding/degenerate-pole machinery self-disables and B-spline faces take the plain-loop path end to end.
```
</details>
